#4320. House House Chose Not To Put Big Walk On Xbox In Accordance With Palestinian-Led BDS Boycott Against Microsoft Games

Untitled Goose Game developer House House launched Big Walk, its multiplayer cooperative puzzle-adventure game, on PlayStation 5, Nintendo Switch 2, PC, and Mac in August to critical acclaim. Since then, players have asked the studio to release the game on Xbox Series X/S, the only console absent from its launch lineup. Today, House House released an explanation stating that Big Walk is not on Xbox in accordance with the Palestinian-led BDS boycott against Microsoft.

"We have made the decision not to release Big Walk on Xbox, in accordance with the Palestinian-led BDS (Boycott, Divestment, Sanctions) movement call to boycott Microsoft, and their designation of Microsoft Games (and Xbox) as a priority boycott target," the statement reads.

House House later writes that it would love for players to experience Big Walk on Xbox, but notes that won't happen until Microsoft and/or Xbox have been removed as BDS boycott targets.

BDS is an international boycott movement "for freedom, justice, and equality," and "upholds the simple principle that Palestinians are entitled to the same rights as the rest of humanity," according to the organization. BDS states that Israel is occupying and colonizing Palestinian land, discriminating against Palestinian citizens of Israel, and denying Palestinian refugees the right to return to their homes. The movement, which has been around since 2005, is inspired by the South African anti-apartheid movement and urges action to pressure Israel to comply with international law, which BDS says the country has been noncompliant with since 1948.

As for the Microsoft and Xbox boycott target prioritization, BDS says on its website that "Microsoft is perhaps the most complicit tech company in Israel's illegal occupation, apartheid regime, and ongoing genocide against 2.3 million Palestinians in Gaza. [...] Microsoft partners with the apartheid regime of Israel and its prison system. It provides the Israeli military with Azure cloud and AI services that are central to accelerating Israel's genocide of 2.3 million Palestinians in the illegally occupied Gaza Strip. After 34 years of deep complicity with Israel's military, the Israeli army relies heavily on Microsoft to meet technological requirements of its genocide and apartheid regime."

The BDS movement also calls on people to boycott Microsoft's gaming products, including canceling Xbox Game Pass subscriptions, boycotting Candy Crush, Minecraft, and Call of Duty, and boycotting all Microsoft Gaming products, including Xbox-branded consoles, headsets, and all games published by Microsoft-owned publishing labels.

House House closes its statement, which you can read in full via the image below, stating, "We've made this decision with the belief that meaningful change can be made in the world, that coordinated collective action can confront even the most powerful interests, and that one day, Palestine will be free."

Big Walk sold one million copies in less than a week. Read Game Informer's Big Walk review here.

#4319. Ancient Ecuadorian genomes reveal 1,500 years of genetic continuity along Pacific coast

Ancient genomes from coastal Ecuador reveal 1,500 years of genetic continuity in an understudied part of the ancient world. John Lindo and colleagues sequenced four genomes from individuals excavated at Salango on Ecuador's central coast, dating to around 2,300 years ago, 1,900 years ago and 740 years ago.

#4318. This Massive Ultra-Thin QLED TV Is 43% Off Right Now

QLEDs are a great mid-tier TV option, sandwiched firmly between cheaper LEDs and premium OLEDs—but there's a wide range within that QLED category, too. The Hisense E7SF QLED is in the low to mid-range of that category, but with its 46% discount, the price is much lower. Amazon currently has the 100-inch Hisense E7SF QLED for $1,697.99 (originally $2,999.99), a new record-low price, according to price-tracking tools. The other sizes are all also seeing discounts, like the 65-inch E7SF QLED, which is down to $599.99 (originally $999.99). These discounts are especially notable given this TV came out in April of 2026.

The first thing you'll notice about the Hisense E7 is how thin and lightweight it is. It comes with four HDMI ports, including one with eARC support for connecting a soundbar and two that support a native 144Hz refresh rate for smoother gaming. Like most new TVs these days, it'll be 4K and have AI modes that improve picture quality. This will be most noticeable when watching sports; the TV will recognize the content and automatically switch the picture settings to sports mode. Because this is a Fire TV, it'll come with built-in Alexa+, which is a nice touch for those already in the Alexa ecosystem. You can also install Kodi to watch virtually anything for free.

Another standout feature is the support for Dolby Vision IQ and Dolby Atmos, which can make your streaming much more immersive as your TV adapts its picture in real time to its environment and gives you a 3D surround-sound experience (if you have a sound system). For its price, this is an excellent choice for someone looking to upgrade their TV to a new 2026 QLED without breaking the bank.

#4317. Resident Evil makers Capcom reveal data breach has affected over 11 million Capcom ID accounts, warn against potential phishing emails

"Credit card information was not included among the affected information," say the publishers

Resident Evil, Street Fighter, and Monster Hunter publishers Capcom have revealed that a data breach has seen a third-party gain access to information associated with more than 11 million Capcom ID accounts. "Suspicious activity" around the service, required to play online in the likes of Street Fighter 6, was initially noticed on October 19th according to Capcom, who have spent the past couple of days taking measures to contain and combat the problem.

Capcom have outlined in a press release that a total of 11,774,247 accounts had their email addresses and "system-specific identifier information" accessed in the breach. 43 of those accounts also had their date of birth, gender, country/region, and nickname records accessed.

"Capcom has not confirmed any compromise of Capcom ID account login passwords originally set by customers themselves," the company wrote. "However, the company has confirmed that passwords were changed without authorisation on 196 accounts. The passwords associated with these accounts have already been reset, and the company continues to investigate the scope of any impact resulting from this incident. Going forward, Capcom will notify customers affected by this incident. Credit card information was not included among the affected information.

As for other advice to affected folks, Capcom issued the following warning regarding the possibility of phishing attacks directed at the email addresses exposed in the breach:

"Email addresses registered with Capcom ID may continue to be used without interruption. However, there is a possibility that the affected information could be misused to send fraudulent emails or other communications impersonating Capcom or related parties. If you receive any suspicious email or message, please exercise caution and refrain from accessing any URLs contained therein or opening any attached files."

Having reported the breach to Osaka's Prefectural Police and Japan’s Personal Information Protection Commission as part of their initial response, which they say "identified and addressed the attack method involved in this incident", Capcom will now plough on with a "detailed investigation" designed to flag measures that might stop this sort of thing happening again in the future.

#4316. Tesla's Cybercab Fixes A Big Repairability Problem With Its Structural Batteries

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

Tesla helped pioneer a shift toward structural battery packs that eliminate traditional modules to save weight and squeeze more energy into a smaller space. But with its latest vehicle, America's EV leader is moving in the other direction in the name of repairability.

Instead of embedding hundreds of in-house 4680 battery cells into a single, tightly integrated assembly, as Tesla did with the structural battery packs found in some Model Ys and the Cybertruck, the Cybercab contains four separate modules of those cells. That means a faulty module can be swapped out without replacing the entire battery pack. Tesla executives explained the decision in a video on YouTube channel of the engineering firm Munro & Associates.

"It's good to be self-critical," said Robert Sumpf, the director of mechanical design engineering for Tesla's battery modules and packs. "I'm going to give our Model Y structural battery a grade D," he added, noting that it was an "awesome product" to the customer but was hard to build.

Tesla 4680 battery cells assembled in a structural pack, without the potting shown.

The other issue with the structural approach is serviceability, he said. Since all the cells are bound together in the pack using a hard potting material, it's practically impossible to repair the battery if one cell is defective, for example. Indeed, after tearing down a Model Y with a structural pack a few years back, Munro & Associates concluded that its battery had "zero repairability."

There are advantages to getting rid of conventional module structures (the building blocks of a battery pack) and making the battery itself structural to the vehicle, in that the design lets manufacturers stuff more cells into a given area and save some material costs. It's why automakers including BMW, Porsche, and Ford are now designing cars with cells that go directly into packs or even directly into the vehicle's body structure.

Tesla's top brass, though, didn't think the serviceability tradeoff was worth it in the Cybercab. Sumpf described the Cybercab's design as "semi-structural." "You're going to get maybe 10% more volume for cells in a structural pack when you delete the structure," Sumpf said. "You're going to save a little cost as a result, but the issue is the service cost."

In general, studies show that EV batteries rarely fail. Degradation is typically predictable and manageable over the lifetime of a consumer EV. However, the Cybercab is supposed to hold up hundreds of thousands of miles of round-the-clock abuse as a robotaxi. That may help explain the decision to switch things up battery-wise. Sure, a structural, module-free pack may help eke out a few extra miles of range, which is great for selling EVs to regular people. But for a fleet-based business, total cost of ownership (TCO) is everything.

"When you think of TCO as we've talked about, replacing an entire pack before half a million miles is a killer on cost," Eric Earley, the Cybercab's chief engineer, told the Munro Live YouTube channel. "So we have to think of [remanufacturing], we have to think of pack serviceability, we have to think of all those things that actually get us to our cost target for the product."

Contact the author: Tim.Levin@InsideEVs.com

#4315. People who've finished Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 on the new YOLO, single-life difficulty share experiences and tips for doing so

The Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 campaign is currently available in early access, and if you're among the many who pre-ordered a copy in order to play it before launch, you've likely noticed a locked difficulty setting that's harder, even, than Realism.

YOLO, which is short for you only live once, is a new difficulty tier introduced in Modern Warfare 4. It does exactly what you expect: gives you a single life to complete the entirety of the campaign. Die once and you're sent back to the start.

A few players have already cleared the Modern Warfare 4 campaign on YOLO, but one in particular took the time to answer a few questions and reveal some of their toughest moments in the campaign.

Reddit user Lost-Equivalent2352 shared a screenshot showing a completed campaign at YOLO difficulty, which they said took them 20 hours. Key to their strategy was, well, a lot of hiding and leaving AI teammates to kill as many enemies as possible, before emerging to finish the rest. They relied on flashbangs to momentarily stun enemies and make it easier to shoot them, but they also used a lot of grenades to clear clusters of enemies.

But that grenade strategy was difficult in one mission - a mission that depicts the chaos of a North Korean invasion of South Korea, and takes place at nighttime. The problem here is that civilians and other allies are spread across the battlefield, so throwing flashbangs and grenades risks damaging either group, ending the YOLO run. Lost-Equivalent2352 only managed to beat it by "pure luck", they said.

Incidentally, Lost-Equivalent2352 played the game using mouse and keyboard, saying: "I think that [it's] impossible to complete it with a controller, you need [good] recoil control in long distances but that's only my opinion."

According to Lost-Equivalent2352, the sections of hand-to-hand combat were among the most difficult, as miss-countering even a single attack can mean the end of the fight, which in turn means the end of the entire run. In those sections, you're unable to use anything but your fists, so there's really no getting around learning the fight.

In order to unlock YOLO, you must first finish the entire campaign on Realism difficulty, which is no easy feat. A YOLO run could end for any reason that shows a game over screen, including accidentally shooting a civilian, failing a jump, losing a chase and so on. Do any of that - or die normally - and you're back to the opening cutscene.

Given the single-life nature of the mode, you can't select missions individually and finish each on YOLO as you can with other difficulty levels, so you really have to do it the way it was intended.

Unfortunately, though, the reward for finishing the campaign on YOLO is underwhelming. It's a Calling Card you can use with your multiplayer profile. Calling Cards are amongst the lowest tier of cosmetic rewards in Call of Duty, so unless someone actually knows how it was acquired, most players probably won't notice.

Modern Warfare 4's DMZ early access begins later today, before the full game - including traditional multiplayer - launches Friday, 23rd October for PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S and Switch 2.

#4314. 2027 Mercedes-Benz EQS SUV

See all results for new 2027 Mercedes-Benz EQS SUV for sale near 315400

The 2027 Mercedes-Benz EQS satisfies all of the key requirements of the mid-size Luxury SUV EV segment: three rows of seating, upscale cabin material choices, and the availability of two dual-motor powertrains: the EQS400 is good for 336 horsepower and 312-miles of EPA- estimated range while the EQS550 pumps out 556 horsepower and delivers 317 miles of range. All-wheel drive is standard, and in C/D testing the 550 hit 60 mph in just 4.0 seconds.

Drill down beneath the surface, however, and you’ll find an overreliance on touchscreen controls, a tight third row, and pricing on the high side of the segment. That said, the interior displays all the familiar Benz styling cues, and the air-spring suspension delivers a smooth, quiet ride—qualities at the top of the want list for many Benz customers.

Expert Tip: While the EQS SUV is still in the hunt, the competition has moved faster in updating their EV offerings. If you wish to stay under the Mercedes umbrella, you can check out the slightly smaller and significantly less expensive EQE EV SUV.

Compare the 2027 Mercedes-Benz EQS SUV with the top vehicles in this segment with our new compare tool.

Very little. The luxury SUV EV adds standard anodized aluminum roof rails and a few new wheel design choices.

If this year's model is largely unchanged and you're comfortable with a lightly used car, you may want to consider shopping previous model years. A one-to-three-year-old model will likely have a warranty and it'll cost less than buying new.

The EQS SUV is essentially a re-bodied EQS sedan with an elevated seating position and more interior space. The rear-drive, single-motor model fell off the table for 2026, so all EQSs are all-wheel drivers. The EQS400 4Matic makes 355 horsepower and 590 pound-feet of torque from its two motors, while the EQS550 4Matic produces a total of 536 horsepower and 633 pound-feet.

All EQS SUVs come with an adjustable air suspension and a rear-axle-steering system that delivers a well-cushioned ride and makes the big people-mover surprisingly easy to maneuver. Handling is capable but not particularly satisfying, and steering feedback is minimal.

Unlike the EQS sedan, the SUV features an Off-Road drive mode that raises the suspension and alters the powertrain's behavior. During a ride-along in a prototype, we experienced this setting on rugged terrain and were impressed with the vehicle's capabilities. The 2026 EQS400 SUV we drove had a relaxed and comfortable on-road demeanor, which is okay by us given its luxury mission.

In our testing, the 2026 EQS400 4Matic recorded a 60-mph time of 5.2 seconds. The previous EQS580 4Matic we tested, which is mechanically identical to the current EQS550 4Matic, recorded a 4.0-second blast to 60 mph.

In Comparison: Power outputs and curb weights in this class vary considerably, which makes comparison tricky. A two-motor Genesis GV60 Performance with 483 horsepower got to 60 in 3.7 seconds, while a 500-hp all-wheel-drive Cadillac Lyriq needed 4.6 seconds to reach that speed.

The EQS SUV can tow up to 3500 pounds when properly equipped.

In Comparison: The Lucid Gravity EV SUV is rated to tug up to 6000 pounds, and the Rivian R1S EV SUV is capable of 7700 pounds.

The 2027 EQS SUVs wield a 118-kWh battery pack that's good for 312 miles of EPA range in the EQS400 4Matic and 317 miles in the more powerful EQS550 4Matic. Both models support DC fast-charging at a rate of up to 200 kilowatts, which will restore the battery’s charge from 10 to 80 percent in about half an hour. In our testing, we were able to charge from 10 percent to 90 percent in 43 minutes.

On our 75-mph highway fuel-economy test route, the 2023 EQS450 4Matic and the EQS580 4Matic both delivered exactly the same 290 miles of range. We'll update this page with results for the latest models when we get the chance to test them.

The EPA rates the EQS450 4Matic at 80 MPGe city, 78 MPGe highway, and 80 MPGe combined, while the EQS580 gets 82, 78, and 79 MPGe, respectively. (MPGe stands for miles per gallon of gas equivalent, an EPA metric that estimates PHEV and EV fuel efficiency.)

On our 75-mph highway fuel-economy route, 2023 versions of the EQS450 4Matic and EQS580 4Matic—which employ slightly smaller battery packs than the current models—saw 78 MPGe and 83 MPGe. For more information about the EQS SUV's fuel economy, visit the EPA's website.

In Comparison: The BMW iX xDrive45 SUV EV has EPA ratings of 94 MPGe city, 93 MPGe highway, and 94 MPGe combined. On our 75-mph highway fuel-economy test route, the iX xDrive45 managed a 99 MPGe result and matched the EQS’s 290 miles of range.

First Row: Inside, the EQS SUV features the type of rich materials and high-tech equipment that define modern-day Mercedes-Benz models. User-adjustable ambient lighting, heated and ventilated front seats, lush leather upholstery, a panoramic sunroof, Benz's dash-spanning Hyperscreen, and wireless charging are included.

Second Row: Riders in the second row have ample leg- and headroom, not to mention standard seat heating, wireless phone charging, and pillow-soft headrests.

Third Row: A two-seat third row is available, but adults will find the wayback too tight. The SUV's sloping roofline eats into headroom back there too.

Cargo Capacity: The cabin has several storage spaces, including a large bin below the floating center console. Cargo volume varies by seat count, with two-row five-seaters offering 31 cubic feet behind the second row and three-row seven-seaters, 28. With all three rows up, cargo capacity drops to just seven cubic feet aft of the last row. In our testing, a two-row EQS SUV held 10 carry-on suitcases in its cargo bay, and a total of 24 with the second-row seats folded.

For maximum theater, the EQS SUV is equipped with Mercedes's mesmerizing 56-inch Hyperscreen, which spans the dash from door to door. Along with housing the digital gauge cluster, it hosts a 17.7-inch touchscreen that's primarily responsible for infotainment functions, plus a 12.3-inch touchscreen in front of the passenger. There's no doubt that the novelty of the glass-covered dash will entice buyers, but we can't help but bemoan the near-total lack of easy-to-use physical switchgear. Every EQS SUV also comes standard with wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto connectivity, a Burmester audio system, and wireless device charging.

The EQS SUV has a host of standard driver-assistance technology. Along with ordinary safety equipment, such as adaptive cruise control and automatic high-beam headlamps, the SUV also has a self-parking feature.

For information about the EQS SUV’s crash-test results, visit the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) and Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS) websites.

Mercedes's warranty periods align with those of other luxury automakers, such as Audi and BMW. However, unlike those German competitors, Benz-branded models don't include any complimentary maintenance.

Each year, we put hundreds of vehicles through our rigorous instrumented testing regimen. If the data in the chart below is for a different model year, that's because this vehicle hasn't changed since we last tested it, and the test results remain accurate.

#4313. Nekome: Nazi Hunter Director On Whether The Game Is Political, Art Inspiration, Metaphysical Themes, Steam Demo, And More

I've been tracking Nekome: Nazi Hunter here at Game Informer since its reveal earlier this year. I finally went hands-on with it during Gamescom 2026 and relished just how disrespectfully brutal it is to Nazis. You will soon get the chance to experience that brutality yourself because developer ProbablyMonsters is releasing a demo on Steam soon on October 19, and it will be available through October 26.

Ahead of that demo launch, ProbablyMonsters also released a new trailer highlighting the game's narrative and villainous Hyena troupe of Nazi leaders as part of today's Galaxies Autumn Showcase. These Hyenas are at the center of protagonist Vano's Nazi hunting, and they are a notorious squad of Nazi agents whose extreme brand of cruelty made them feared across occupied Europe and the world, according to a press release. Players will control Vano as he kills countless Nazis in violent and bloody ways while tracking down each of the Hyenas (who he will presumably also kill violently and bloodily).

Check it out in the latest Nekome: Nazi Hunter trailer for yourself below:

Game Informer also spoke to Nekome: Nazi Hunter game director Jeronimo Barrera about the game in the lead-up to this trailer and the incoming Steam Next Fest demo. In the interview, which took place over email, Barrera talks about the game's politics (or lack thereof), the Hyenas, visual inspiration, and more:

Game Informer's Wesley LeBlanc: How did the team decide on the Hyenas as the antagonists/villains of the game, rather than say, the obvious choice of Hitler?

Barrera: As we developed the narrative, we had already committed to pursuing the grindhouse aesthetic that would drive the presentation. We needed a disgusting set of villains that would be fun to hunt down and murder. Although we are rooted in real-world events, the player fantasy we are after is to hunt down and murder as many Nazis, both big and small, and that needs to deliver over-the-top fun.

Can you tell me about the Hyenas? What are their differences from a narrative perspective, and what are their differences from a gameplay perspective? Does each Hyena approach the hunt for Vano in different ways?

We introduce the Hyenas almost immediately in the narrative. After that, the player won’t be confused as to why they need to hunt them down, not the other way around! Vano hunts them down, and each Hyena boss fight is a unique challenge that pushes the players' abilities.

It sounds like Vano will be hunted across occupied Europe and beyond? What can you tell me about that beyond?

Our research into how we can tell a new story and give a new experience has given us a lot of surprising locations to explore. For example, we were shocked when we realized that we could have our first encounters with Nazis on U.S. soil! Vano’s adopted home is New York City. It’s hard to believe, but there were Nazi Party rallies in New York. This is where Vano starts his hunt through a tragic coincidence, which then takes Vano to Europe. But he’s not being hunted; he is the hunter.

Is there anything larger than life or supernatural or demonic happening narratively, or is it Vano versus some Nazis that are led by the Hyenas? I ask because scenes in this new trailer almost imply some monstrous transformation, or maybe even something vampiric, as blood drips from the Hyenas' faces (though I recognize that could just be a style choice).

The Nazis were hideous monsters. We play on those themes for our abilities and how we present them to the player. There are themes we explore that could be considered metaphysical. But with that said, it’s up for people to interpret the narrative on their own.

How would you describe the art style/visual design of the game?

Our biggest inspiration came from the grindhouse film genre. Turning the stylized pulpiness and violence of movies like Inglourious Basterds into a video game was a challenge that the team was excited to tackle, and we believe this grindhouse spirit gives our game a distinct visual style.

Graphic novels were another major influence. Our goal with the art style was to create something over the top and entertaining, while respecting the serious subject matter. The game would lose its sense of fun if it looked too real, and graphic novels helped us find a look that is visually interesting yet able to handle the story’s darkness and violence.

How long has the team been working on the game?

The team began working on the game in 2024. The genesis of the game was a meeting inside ProbablyMonsters where we started quoting Inglourious Basterds, and we all became set on the idea of making a game about hunting down and killing Nazis through an untold Second World War story.

Nekome: Nazi Hunter’s development is led internally at ProbablyMonsters by Brett Bourbin, Brand Inman, Sergei Kuprejanov, Kay Willis and me, with assistance from co-development partners.

What has it been like working on a smaller title like this and what does your AAA experience bring to the project?

Going from concept to the player’s hands in 18 months is an epic lift that’s only possible with amazing devs that bring extraordinary experience and expertise. The AAA sensibilities run deep at ProbablyMonsters; there are some legends in our house. What makes us different is that our independent approach allows us to tell unique stories and make experiences like Nekome that would have a hard time getting to market.

After Gamescom, ButWhyTho? published a story about how Willis said the game isn’t political, and it caused a lot of discussion/backlash about the game. I wanted to see if the team had any follow-up on the idea that Nekome isn’t political now that some time has passed. At least from my perspective, all art is political; not to mention, making a game where you’re very much visibly and graphically killing and mutilating Nazis is sending a very clear message, especially in 2026 when we have people proudly stating they are a Nazi today. But that’s just my line of thinking – I’m curious where the team lands on this discussion now that some additional time has passed.

I think we're overcomplicating this. Nazis are bad. That shouldn't be a political statement, just common sense. Nekome is a revenge story about people who suffered horrific atrocities at the hands of Nazis. We're not making a statement about modern politics. We're telling a story with villains who are unquestionably evil.

If someone feels politically attacked by a game where you kill Nazis, maybe they should take a long, hard look in the mirror.

Anything I missed you’d like to touch on?

We created Nekome because we saw an opportunity to tell a Second World War story through a perspective that games have rarely depicted. World War II is one of the most common settings in media. And yet, there are almost no games that explore the persecution of the Roma and Sinti during that period.

That is a real, deeply horrible part of history, so our story also comes with responsibility. The team has worked with historians and cultural consultants to help us approach Vano’s identity, his community and the historical context thoughtfully and credibly. We promise to tell Vano’s story honestly and approach that history with care.

#4312. Rice plants can fight off devastating disease after gene editing with AI-designed tool

University of Missouri researchers have created rice plants that resist bacterial blight, a devastating disease that threatens one of the world's most important food crops. The advance could help farmers in parts of Asia and Africa, where the disease can destroy harvests and threaten livelihoods.

#4311. The Sims 4 developer hasn't forgotten about its quality of life commitment, and is back with an update on what to expect in 2026

Maxis has offered some fresh details on its long-running mission to improve player quality of life in The Sims 4.

As the The Sims 4 continues into its second decade, the volume of new content, major expansions, and long-awaited features has grown in lockstep. Unfortunately, that has also meant that, given how old the game is, a few technical problems remain a nuisance.

The effort required to fix them is what led developer Maxis to make a commitment to improve the game’s technical state, and the developer has now returned to update the community on what to expect in 2026.

The quality of life pledge was made in September last year, through the introduction of a roadmap. Since then, the developer issued one update on its progress. Which brings us to this week’s new blog post, which further builds on everything that’s come before.

In the post, Maxis revealed a few key areas it’s going to be focusing on going forward. Though specific release dates for these changes were not shared, the list of issues should resonate with many players, as it covers many often-discussed topics.

The first stage of the new roadmap covers March through August. Over that period, you can expect Sims' autonomy to improve. This goes for things like how they choose locations for certain actions, they way they interact with certain objects, and even how often they interrupt conversations.

The infant and caregiver relationship is being updated, too, in order to improve autonomy and make sure your little Sims don’t miss out on milestones and interactions. That stage of the roadmap also includes fixes for black photo issues, and other crashes and freezing.

The next period takes place September through December, and it includes more interesting changes. One of the goals with this one is to improve Sims’ dining experience, which covers issues around eating and drinking.

Another important area of focus with this stage is family trees, and relationships between members of the same family. Maxis wants to improve the game’s genealogy systems to make relationship tracking across generations more robust so that your family history isn’t lost.

Depending on how the first stage goes, you might see more issues join the list of what the developer is looking at for the second stage of the roadmap.

The good news is that you won’t have to wait too long for the game’s next patch. Due out in March, this next update will feature around 55 fixes, including for some commonly reported issues.

Until then, you may want to check out The Sims 4’s Lost Legacies Event, which is available now until the middle of March. If you picked up the game’s latest expansion, check if you tried those Royalty & Legacy cheats.

#4310. "It just seemed arbitrary, and not very smart": EA was never a responsible owner for Bioware, but the developer never seemed to learn from their mistakes

19 years after buying Bioware, Electronic Arts have closed the veteran RPG developer. But as saddening as this is, bringing an end to series such as Dragon Age and Mass Effect, EA have never had an easy relationship with the studio.

Looking through old interviews with developers who worked across Baldur's Gate, Neverwinter Nights, Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic, Dragon Age, Mass Effect, and Anthem, the corporation immediately changed the atmosphere of the studio and never seemed to understand what exactly they had bought, or even why.

However, another thread of the conversations is that from project to project, Bioware repeated the same mistakes, getting years into projects before working out what they were trying to make. Sometimes they could pull an exceptional game out of this mess, but other times it led to costly missteps like Anthem.

Back when I was running TechRadar Gaming, features editor Jeremy Peel worked with Jon Bailes to produce the oral history of Mass Effect. I'm hideously biased, but it's an excellent account of that game's development. It was during the RPG's creation that EA acquired Bioware, and developers on the game immediately noticed a change.

"What I noticed once EA came in was how the language changed at work, and started adopting more corporate-isms, like the language of corporate America," audio design lead Steve Sim recalled. "At one time if you needed help on a project, you'd say, 'I need more guys,' or 'I need more people to help me out with this.' And then it turned into, 'I need more resources.' So the biggest change I found was just the change in the language, especially amongst producers and that type of crowd. They were assimilating into the Borg, it seemed."

It's the kind of language that you will find through the open letter published by EA's chief operating officer David Tinson and chief studios officer Cam Weber. The first reference to Bioware's closure is tied up in the euphemism "Saying no to some things we're passionate about". Doing so, they say, "allows us to focus our teams and resources so we give our biggest opportunities everything they need to succeed."

Also speaking in the Mass Effect oral history, director of technology and Bioware co-founder Trent Oster describes the immediate costcutting EA enacted on taking over the studio. "When we were acquired we were all looking forward to it like, 'Yeah, we're owned by EA, they're going to give us a budget, we finally don't have to keep constraining ourselves and crushing things down and cutting corners.' And one of the first things they did was like, 'OK, based on your budgets, we need to cut across the company 10%.' And we were like, 'Oh, so we're not getting more, we're getting less.' That was a bit of the EA feeling. We call it the EA butter knife. It just seemed arbitrary, and not very smart."

The corporate cost cutting Oster describes took place nearly 20 years ago, but it continued throughout EA's ownership. Most recently, in 2023 when 50 staff were laid off to “shift [Bioware] towards a more agile and more focused studio”. Then in 2025, more staff were laid off, this time it was described as "taking [the] opportunity between full development cycles to reimagine how we work at BioWare".

It becomes particularly clear with Anthem, Bioware's launched-but-quickly-shuttered live-service game, how EA's influence manifested at the studio. Interviewed in the Summer Eternal Anthology, long-time Bioware writer David Gaider explains the design concept for the game seemed to be formed from executive demands. "The nearest I could tell, working in a corporate environment, was that it was a sifted-down list of bullet-point requirements, that no single person ever took responsibility for," Gaider explains. "It just kind of happened like that. There needs to be this project, so it was willed into existence, but nobody would say that before this project is actually kicked off, someone needs to take responsibility for the vision."

Mark Darrah echoes this in his own interview in the anthology. "Casey pitched the exact thing [EA execs] wanted to hear," he says. "Always online, live service, infinite revenue. Multiplayer." Darrah describes it as "a near-perfect pitch deck." But, he also questions whether "Bioware is the company to make a live service looter shooter."

However, while those ideas may have filtered down from EA and then been pitched back to them by Bioware leadership, it meant that work would begin on a project long before it had crystalised into a clear premise. "The idea was that they could kick the project off, and that the designer, the design department, or the creative director, particularly, would then create the vision," Gaider says. "But at that point, the project's already happening. So each time it felt like trying to catch a bronco bull who's already out and running and bucking, and you're just trying to keep up."

For that reason, Gaider claims "Anthem was doomed from the start."

Development on Anthem began in 2012, but when Gaider joined the project 18 months later, it was struggling. "The team on it was just going around in circles," Gaider says. They didn't know what they wanted." Hudson then left Bioware in 2014 and Darrah says "they basically ran with no leadership, for two, three years." In 2017 Hudson returned and Darrah was brought over from the team working on the next Dragon Age project. Over the next two years Anthem took shape before it eventually released in 2019, but, in doing so, it meant the Dragon Age team was also then left without leadership for the next two years.

"My biggest regret," Darrah says, "is I kind of feel like I should have told EA 'no' when they asked me to take over Anthem. I should have just let whatever was going to happen, happen at that point." He adds that leaving "Dragon Age running without an executive producer from 2017 to 2019 was bad. That was a huge mistake."

Darrah says he doesn't feel like he had a choice, however, because "EA was very good at making Bioware feel like things were very dire." Though, he points to the fact that Hudson's replacement as Bioware's general manager, Gary McKay, was able to delay Dragon Age: Veilguard through multiple financial years. "That tells me either he's magic, which is possible, or it was never quite as 'gun to our head' as we thought it was," Darrah says.

(Bear in mind Darrah was being interviwed ahead of Bioware's closure and he's referring to events in the past five years.)

However, while pressure from EA may have significantly impacted the development and direction of Bioware's games, it appears that the studio repeated the same mistake for decades. Gaider, Darrah, and the developers that contributed to the Mass Effect oral history, all say Bioware consistently got deep into projects without having a vision of what they were trying to build.

"When we started, Dragon Age's big premise was 'It's D&D, but it's not'," Gaider says in his Summer Eternal interview. "We'll just file the ID numbers enough to make it our own. We went through a long period of going around in circles on Dragon Age." Edwin's covered this as a push and pull between teams that thought they were making Conan, others thinking it was Lord of the Rings, and still others thinking they were making a new Diablo.

Gaider believes the only reason EA allowed Bioware to complete the original Dragon Age was because the team "were already halfway through development, and [EA] considered it sunk cost. They said, 'Well, you've got to finish this.' But it was clear that Electronic Arts considered Dragon Age, our initial vision, to be too old fashioned, too slow, and too ponderous."

Gaider says Mass Effect had the benefit of Casey Hudson, who was able to pull together a single vision for the project much sooner. Though this may be something of a grass is greener situation, because the developers on Mass Effect describe being on the game for three years before it started coming together and even then it was only because they had to prepare something for E3.

"It was the first time we pulled together playable moments in the engine that really proved to us what the game was capable of," Hudson says in the oral history. "Until you pull things together, it's all sort of theoretical, and you think you know what game you're making. But once you have that moment in time, where everything's pulled together, you know exactly what the game needs to be."

It was only after the vertical slice was completed, lead designer Preston Watamaniuk says, "that the team understood what we were building and was able to then work autonomously, without as much hands-on direction."

This approach continued through the years. In 2012 Bioware began work on Mass Effect: Andromeda. Three years into development they scrapped the plans and tech they were using to procedurally generate worlds switching to a more traditional style of RPG, making the bulk of the game in its final 18 months. In 2015 they started work on a their next Dragon Age project, which was shelved in 2017 in favour of the project that would become Veilguard, but that would then take another seven years to complete, with a live service component first adopted from Anthem and then dropped after Anthem's failure.

It looks to have still been a problem for the studio with its current Mass Effect project. First announced back in 2020, initially as part of the "N7 Day" and then followed up with a teaser trailer at The Game Awards, the new Mass Effect was still described in EA's open letter as in "early-development". Developers who worked on the cancelled game are saying they believe they were "making something special", and aimed build on what they saw coming out of Larian and CD Projekt Red, but six years is a long time to still be in early development and sounds a lot like the project could have fallen into the "going around in circles" Darrah described of Dragon Age and Anthem.

I am saddened by Bioware's closure. The team have put out incredible games over the decades, and it sounds like they were never a natural match for EA's executives or the corporation's goals. However, it also sounds like the studio have continued to face the same challenges on projects year after year without really learning how to avoid them.

Still, it would have been much better that they be allowed to spin off and become independent or be sold to another publisher who knows what the talented team can create, than to be closed down after years of staff layoffs and project cancellations.

#4307. Helldivers 2 swaps over to Easy anti-cheat, with developers admitting Gameguard "wasn't as effective as it could have been"

Helldivers will be switching up its anti-cheat, as developer Arrowhead Game Studios has admitted that its Gameguard cheat policy "wasn't as effective as it could have been."

In a post made to the game's Steam page (not available in the UK - get that VPN on), the team explains the decision: "The implementation of GameGuard for Helldivers 2 wasn't as effective as it could have been [...] In the end we decided to take another path with EAC. EAC is an industry standard used in over 200 games, and we believe this will be an effective way to protect the Galactic War and will better protect fair gameplay and the integrity of the game."

As part of this shift, Arrowhead also confirmed that several non-malicious mods, such as cosmetic changes, will need to be updated to work alongside Easy Anti-Cheat. "We know mods are part of the DNA of PC gaming and gaming communities as a whole. In general, we appreciate the creativity behind cosmetic and non-gameplay mods, which players can enjoy without negatively impacting those who play alongside them," the post reads.

"Where we need to improve is how we protect players against modifications that create unfair advantages or negatively impact others, which goes beyond software manipulation to tamper with our game. These changes mean that some mods, particularly the types that are affecting the experience of other players, may no longer function."

The post continues to implore those who feel a non-malicious mod has been blocked by Easy Anti-Cheat to reach out via Zendesk. So, there should be a bit of a rough transitional period, but hopefully a lot of the fun mods should be good to go again in the near future.

It's indeed true that Easy Anti-Cheat has become the norm for a vast variety of PC games. However, regardless of the anti cheat being used, those in the UK are still unable to play Helldivers 2 due to a mysterious issue. Sony has promised to address the issue with Valve, but as of writing there's been no resolution to the problem.

#4306. Eternal Anima Hands-On Preview – Strive How I Want To

Eternal Anima is the time-twisting RPG revealed during the big September Nintendo Direct that looked like a Square Enix HD-2D game but isn’t. It is also the game with the “Never Strive For A Better Tomorrow” splash screen. After playing the game for roughly an hour within developer Marvelous Inc.’s Tokyo office, I eschew that line – I will, in fact, strive for a better tomorrow, if only so I can play more Eternal Anima one day.

The world has essentially come to its final act in Eternal Anima. Urvis Ael is the last remaining bastion of civilization in a flooded world. That said, living in Urvis Ael isn’t all that bad; just don’t strive for a better tomorrow or really any kind of change. Protagonist Wade breaks those rules and sets out on an adventure, feeling deep down that a better life is possible.

Wade is bestowed with the power of Psychometry, which lets him look into the past, view memories, and inherit abilities and classes for use in combat. My hands-on time with Eternal Anima is mostly focused on combat, and for good reason: it rules. It is turn-based combat, but with a time-turning twist. Wade also has the power of Future Sight, which means he already knows exactly what every enemy will do each turn, reflected in the UI. Every ability has an action speed shown on the timeline at the bottom of the screen. You can always see an enemy’s upcoming action, who they will target, and even the outcome.

Combat in Eternal Anima is less about hitting enemies harder and more about hitting them smarter. When you attack an enemy's weaknesses with Wade or other party members, you knock them back on the battle timeline, pushing their predecided actions back in the move queue. Your goal is to push them back enough that you kill them before they can attack, or, for bigger enemies, knock them off the timeline in what’s known as a Break. This cancels their action, opening up your party for another round of attacks.

The battle system doesn’t feel as active as a standard turn-based RPG; instead, it makes you feel more like a god playing with action figures. You decide what happens, then sit back and watch it all unfold.

Everything is depicted in a beautiful, bokeh-enhanced art style that’s unique to Eternal Anima, despite the initial HD-2D impression. I love how the world and enemies look, which makes it even more disappointing that the characters are rendered as chibi. It doesn’t gel with the game’s painterly visuals and clashes with its heavier thematic tones.

As I explore Eternal Anima’s miniature settings, which resemble closed-off dioramas placed carefully on a larger world map, I take part in fishing minigames, collect items, defeat enemies, and encounter NPCs from the past, like Fletcher. After I interact with a glowing sword on the ground, an animated flashback plays, detailing Fletcher’s story and ending with a notification that I received a bow from his past.

As I continue through this world, some two centuries before my time, I encounter a man from this period who’s trying to save his forest hometown from flooding. Of course, I already know this will happen in my timeline because the entire world is flooded, but Wade suspects that if he can convince this person of the looming threat, he can change the present by altering the past. My demo ends shortly after, but now, a month removed from the preview, I’m still thinking about Eternal Anima, specifically its combat.

A lot of turn-based combat makes you feel powerful. But Eternal Anima’s makes you feel like a genius, and that’s smart and novel. I’m left striving for March now.

#4303. Genshin Impact fans on PS4 only have six weeks to find another platform as support winds down with one final update

Premium in-game purchases have now been disabled on PlayStation 4, as one of Genshin's four original launch platforms prepares to get dropped in early April.

Version Luna 5 of Genshin Impact is now live, but it's a bittersweet occasion for fans of HoYoverse's breakout gacha RPG – at the very least, those who are still playing the game on PlayStation 4, which is presumably true of somebody out there.

HoYoverse announced last year that they intended to wind down support for Genshin Impact on PS4, which – alongside PC, Android, and iOS – was one of the launch platforms for the game back in 2020. And with Version Luna 5, those end days are upon us.

The game has already been delisted on the platform since last September, meaning it was no longer available for download and could only be updated by players who already had it installed. But as of today, paid purchases from the in-game store have been disabled on PS4; and in six weeks' time, on approximately April 8, Version Luna 6 will discontinue access to the PS4 version altogether.

In many ways this is unsurprising – Genshin is already available on both PS5 and Xbox Series to sate fans eager to play on console, and the PS4's hardware is increasingly struggling to keep up with this massive open-world game after more than five years of regular updates have ballooned both its file size and technical requirements.

Aptly enough, Luna 5 takes the main story action back to the nation of Mondstadt, the location where the Traveler (and, by extension, the players) took their first steps into the world of Teyvat back in September 2020. It feels like a symbolic last hurrah for the original console home of the game, as does the inclusion of a Chronicled Wish banner focussing on some of Mondstadt's most iconic (read: too old to be meta by now) 5-Star characters – namely Eula, Mona, Albedo, Klee, Diluc, and Jean.

And as the icing on the Tea Break Pancake, this banner is running alongside the long-anticipated introduction of Varka – the Grand Master of the Knights of Favonius who has been a major character in the game's lore from the very beginning, but who only became playable with today's update.

Looking ahead to that post-Luna 6 future, Genshin Impact will still be available on PS5, as well as Xbox Series consoles, PC, and mobile devices – so hopefully anyone who is still playing primarily on PS4 will find another platform they can continue the adventure on by April.

#4302. Tozo NC9 Pro review - "Hi-tech case with some good features"

The TOZO NC9 Pro has some very interesting features for a pair of headphones, especially at this price point. This pair of earbuds has a really interesting case, which has a touch screen and acts a lot like a phone. But before we dive into that, let's talk about the earbuds themselves.

The TOZO NC9 Pro is a small set of earbuds that have a thicker bit to them, coming out towards the bottom of your ear. These are heavier than my normal pair of earbuds, and as such, I didn't find them as comfortable. I do prefer more lightweight fixings on mine, but that is a personal preference. There are a few pairs of ear nubs within the box, so that you can adjust them to the size that you prefer for your ears.

When you first start setting up the earbuds, they are easy to connect via Bluetooth by holding down a small, flush button on the back of the charging case, with the top open. They then appear on your phone! Before doing this, however, your case does prompt you to set it up. You'll pick the language you want to see the case in, and a QR code appears asking you to download the TOZO app on your phone.  TOZO NC3 review - "Budget earbuds with good qualities!" If you do download this app, you can customise your own EarTune sound personalisations, so that the sound sounds the way you'd like it to be. There is also LDAC support to allow for higher-quality Bluetooth audio. Beyond sound-related changes, the TOZO App has AI translation, where you can listen to someone speaking to you in another language and then translate it on the fly, instantly, so you can keep the conversation going. This supports over 145 different languages, which is a wild advancement! You can also use the earbuds to take meeting notes during a class or meeting, if that's a feature you look forward to.

The case itself has a slider bar to unlock, the time, and how charged both the case and the earbuds are when they are placed in. Sliding to unlock the case does give you some settings: volume, sound mode, find your earbuds - that sort of thing, which is a nice touch. With all of that said, however, the TOZO NC9 Pro case does feel on the flimsy side, particularly with the lid. It just doesn't feel like it is made of a more durable plastic.

The earbuds themselves do have good water durability, with them being IPX8 water resistance, so that you can use them to work out without any sort of issues. When it comes to the battery life of this pair of earbuds, you can find yourself having 14 hours with noise cancelling off and about 10 hours with it on. The case holds 60 hours of charge itself as well.

#4301. Xbox reportedly mandate four days a week in office for staff close to Microsoft's Washington HQ, up from three day requirement brought in last year

Change set to take effect from beginning of February next year, it's claimed

Xbox have reportedly informed staff that, from the start of February 2027, those within commuting distance of Microsoft's Washington base will have to come into the office four days a week. This follows the mandating of three days in office last year, and comes as the company are looking at cutting more staff as part of ongoing efforts to eventually get rid of 3200 people in multi-round mass layoffs.

"Starting February 1st, 2027, Xbox employees in Puget Sound who live within 50 commuting miles of Studio D are required to work from the office a minimum of four days a week," Xbox's chief people officer Jacinda Chislum Pearson wrote in an internal memo seen by The Verge.

Pearson continued that the company have "thousands of Xbox employees within commuting distance of Redmond" and couched the move as being about the idea that "working together in person makes it easier to review work, resolve issues, and make decisions without the burden of scheduling another meeting".

The Verge report that the switch to four days in office will only apply to staff deemed to live close enough to Microsoft's Redmond campus and cite the memo as suggesting "established in-office practices" at Xbox-owned studios won't change. Though, the site do note that Xbox staff who've already been given or agreed exemptions to Microsoft's previous return to office mandates will need to resubmit for them. Last September, Microsoft internally announced plans to bring in rules mandating staff within 50 miles of the Washington campus come into the office three days a week, with those rules coming into force in February of this year.

It doesn't take a genius to look at the situation and predict that Xbox staff seem likely to have a strong chance of getting a memo around this time next year demanding they prepare to begin commuting in all five days of the working week.

While this move might turn out to have been planned well in advance of Microsoft's current spate of layoffs and studio rejigging, it's not surprising to see a company reportedly planning to cut more people loose in the coming months pushing out new rules affecting how remaining staff have to go about their jobs. Make people's working lives less convenient and you make even those who were previously content more likely to seek opportunities elsewhere, if there are any decent ones to be had, or to just leave.

As of writing, Xbox parent company Microsoft are also still subject to a BDS movement boycott over their reported ties to the Israeli military.

Anbernic is quickly becoming a portable problem

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anbernic is undeniably one of the best retro handheld makers around, but for 3DS emulator devs like Azahar, it's flying too close to the piracy solicitation sun. In a series of posts tied to the portable maker's latest device, the clamshell RG DDS, the team has denounced decisions to use "unofficial piracy-focused forks" of the software in gameplay advertising, in turn distancing itself from the brand.

In an X thread, the Azahar Emulator team confirms, "It has come to our attention that handheld manufacturer Anbernic has been promoting their latest handheld device, the RG DDS, using unofficial piracy-focused forks of Azahar, and has been openly featuring these forks in their advertising material." The trailer in question uses Azahar Plus to run games like Pokemon Ultra Sun, Resident Evil Revelations, Monster Hunter Generations Ultimate, Zelda: Ocarina of Time 3D, and Kirby's Blowout Blast.

The idea of an emulator team turning its back on a handheld maker for playing games with its emulator might be a head-scratcher. However, Azahar says, "After some internal discussion, we have decided that we will NOT be officially supporting this device or others from this manufacturer: Any issues or bugs related to devices from this specific manufacturer will not be investigated or resolved."

It has come to our attention that handheld manufacturer Anbernic has been promoting their latest handheld device, the RG DDS, using unofficial piracy-focused forks of Azahar, and has been openly featuring these forks in their advertising material.October 26, 2026

Azahar goes a step further, saying, "If you would like to stay on a device with guaranteed official support, we recommend looking for alternatives." Simply put, the developer reckons you should opt for something like the Retroid Pocket Duo. Again, some of you may feel this reaction is disproportionate, but it makes sense, since Anbernic could end up on the US Notorious Markets list alongside other ROM-loaded handhelds.

I should stress that Anbernic isn't necessaily the one selling handhelds with unauthorized ROMs included, as it's usually third-party sellers who will explicitly use the devices for piracy. That said, the portable maker is doing itself zero favors by demoing its hardware using anything Nintendo, and outright showing the world that Azahar can run Ocarina of Time right before the remake drops could place the dev team under Ninty's litigious spotlight.

Keep in mind that Azahar was born out of the back of Citra, a renowned 3DS emulator that was closed down alongside its Yuzu Switch emulator sibling following a $2.4 million settlement with Nintendo. That more than explains why devs are now treading with extreme caution, and with the Entertainment Software Association already calling Anbernic out specifically, the emulator could end up being a patsy for the new portable.

Sailing the seven seas of video game piracy has always been a fine line. In fact, the only reason players are able to play their legally owned backups on other devices at all is down to old precedents set by historic court cases (like Bleem! vs. Sony). Technically speaking, it's all still a grey area, and the last thing the scene wants is Nintendo finally bringing a party to court to set a new precedent.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

The reason Azahar should watch its back all comes down to the liability of software vs. hardware. Technically speaking, Anbernic could argue that its device isn't exclusively designed for running Nintendo 3DS games, despite looking almost exactly like the 2000s clamshell. On the other hand, emulators are more or less the tools that allow people to pirate games in the first place, meaning they are likely to face heavier legal repercussions than a handheld maker.

I've been covering the retro console and handheld scene for a long time, and I'd argue Anbernic isn't doing anything out of the ordinary with its RG DDS marketing. At the same time, Azahar is right to distance itself from anything that could be construed as piracy, even if the art of running your own game backups with enhanced resolutions and settings is more nuanced. The big N doesn't deem running its games on anything that isn't officially branded hardware legal, and that'll become a real issue as handhelds and emulators evolve.

Swing by the best gaming handhelds for souped up portable PCs that can run your Steam library on the go.

In their spare time, they pour hours into fixing old consoles, modding Game Boys, exploring ways to get the most out of the Steam Deck, and blasting old CRT TV visuals into their eye sockets.

#4299. Four Ways the Fitbit Edge Is Better Than the Charge 6, and Two Ways It Isn’t

The Fitbit brand has long been defined by fitness trackers with tiny screens, yet it's been three years since it released a new one. Now that the Fitbit Edge has been announced (and pre-orders have opened), longtime Fitbit users are probably wondering how it compares to the last Fitbit release with a screen, the Charge 6.

The design is the first clear difference: The Edge fits in perfectly between the screenless Fitbit Air and the smooth, curved screen of the Pixel Watch. It runs on the same operating system as the Fitbit Air, which is slightly different from the OS that runs on Pixel watches. Like other Fitbits, the Edge pairs with both iPhones and Android devices. Now, let's look at what Google has added since the Charge 6—and what it took away.

I just unboxed the Fitbit Edge that Google sent me to review, and can confirm: There is a physical button on the right side of the casing. It makes an audible click when you press it in, even when the device is turned off. The Charge 6 has a "button" that is just a haptic nub—squeeze it, and if you're lucky it might recognize your touch and buzz in response. (Sadly, I found it often didn't work.)

The altimeter is a brand-new bit of hardware in the Fitbit Edge. You have to go back to the Charge 4 to find a Fitbit tracker with an altimeter. This is the sensor that tells you your altitude and enables a measurement of how many "floors" you have climbed throughout the day. It also allows for more accurate tracking of hills on your hikes and hilly runs.

The Charge 6 and the Edge both have GPS capabilities, allowing the device to know where you are in the world, even if you left your phone at home. (The Fitbit Air, by contrast. does not have its own GPS and has to pester your phone to find out where you are and how fast you're running.) The Charge 6's GPS has some serious flaws, the main one being that tightening the wristband too much could strain the GPS antenna, leading to comically distorted maps, as you can see from my testing. Google says the Fitbit Edge has better GPS hardware. I believe them, but mainly because it would be hard for it to be worse.

This is the biggest downgrade: With the Charge 6, you could set up Google Wallet and be able to tap your Fitbit to pay for things at stores. That's a convenient feature if you like to run or walk without your phone and still be able to buy a drink at a convenience store. But the Fitbit Edge dropped this capability, probably to make room for other internal components. If you loved Google Pay on your older Fitbit, you'll miss it if you upgrade to the Edge.

The Charge 6's music player is one of the most frustrating things about it. The music controls only work for Youtube Music. That's a premium service, meaning you need to pay a monthly subscription fee just to be able to control music playing on your phone, and it has to be a subscription to Youtube Music—Spotify or Apple Music users are out of luck.

Fortunately, that's been fixed in the Fitbit Edge. Google says it will be able to control whatever is playing on your phone (like pretty much every other watch in existence) rather than relying on a specific app.

At launch, the Fitbit Edge doesn't support Google Maps directions—but Google's spec sheets say it's "coming soon." To be fair, Google Maps directions were never particularly useful. They lived in their own little app, and only gave you turn-by-turn directions, not a map view showing where you are. In the long run, this isn't likely a difference that matters, since Google plans to bring this feature to the Edge. But if you enjoy it on your Charge 6, you should know it's not going to be available right away on the Fitbit Edge.

#4298. Ex-Call of Duty designer "who survived the crunch" in order to launch Black Ops 1 & 2 calls out executives "raking it in" from sales of remasters

"Highlights a fundamental flaw in how our industry values its talent."

A former long-time Call of Duty developer has called out Activision executives profiteering from the re-release of games like Black Ops 1 and 2, whereas the developers who contributed "blood, sweat, and tears" to make them gained nothing.

Gavin Goslin worked at Treyarch for more than 15 years as a level artist and level designer, and in a LinkedIn post reacting to low effort re-releases of Black Ops 1 & 2 this year, said: "They are printing money off of work completed over a decade ago.

"As a developer who actually worked on both BO1 and BO2, seeing these charts is bittersweet. I poured my life into those games, spending countless, brutal hours in crunch alongside an incredibly talented team to make sure they were perfect. Seeing our blood, sweat, and tears still dominating the charts in 2026 is amazing, but it's hard to watch the suits rake in giant financial windfalls from our old code while the actual creators don't see a dime of it."

The post was subsequently picked up by various gaming websites - a recirculation Goslin reacted to in a separate post on LinkedIn today. "I am glad this critical conversation is gaining traction," Goslin said.

"To watch executives rake in massive financial windfalls off our old code, while the actual creators face unemployment and don't see a dime of that new compensation, highlights a fundamental flaw in how our industry values its talent," Goslin added. "If legacy games are going to print money for publishers in 2026, legacy rewards and protections need to exist for the developers who survived the crunch to build them."

We have asked Activision for comment about these claims.

The Call of Duty: Black Ops 1 & 2 re-releases, released in July, were reportedly a huge success for Activision and owner Microsoft, selling something like 11 million copies and raking in nearly $450m. This is despite the fact they are not remasters but simple re-releases. US data-tracker Circana not only highlighted Black Ops 1 & 2 as the highest earning game in July, but called it the fifth highest-earning game of the year. It's a remarkable success for simply re-issuing these roughly 15-year-old games.

Meanwhile, the new Call of Duty game, Modern Warfare 4, prepares for release this Friday, 23rd October, buoyed by a very positive reaction to its campaign, by players with pre-order early access to the game. We're in the process of reviewing the game and will share our thoughts soon.

#4297. Chevy Traverse Will Reportedly Get GM’s New Hybrid Powertrain

The same goes for GM’s other mid-size family SUVs, the Buick Enclave and GMC Acadia.

Right now, the only hybrid model that GM makes is the Chevy Corvette, which is pretty wild when you think about it. However, that will change in the coming years, as the automotive giant today announced it's preparing the Lansing Delta Township assembly plant to build future hybrid models. What will those models be exactly? The highly popular Chevy Traverse, Buick Enclave, and GMC Acadia will get hybrid versions, according to Reuters, which cited anonymous sources familiar with the situation.

See all results for new 2027 Chevrolet Traverse for sale near p6a 2a1

While GM announced it plans to invest $1.25 billion in the factory, including over $2 billion in the Lansing area over the next several years, it didn’t explicitly confirm what models will receive the hybrid powertrain or disclose any details about the system. However, the Lansing Delta Township Assembly is where the Acadia, Enclave, and Traverse are all currently built, so it makes sense that they’ll be the first to get the hybrid treatment. Last year, GM reported the highest production numbers in the factory’s 20-year history, as more than 291,000 vehicles rolled off the assembly line.

The General’s strategic shift to hybrid powertrains comes after its big investments in fully electric vehicles failed to pay off due to a mix of tepid adoption rates and the Trump administration nixing federal tax credits for both EVs and plug-in hybrid vehicles. On top of that, gas prices in the United States have remained high, and GM’s metaphorical cupboard of fuel-efficient models is bare compared with other major automakers such as Honda, Hyundai, and Toyota, and even Ford.

GM also announced that part of the money that it's investing in the Lansing factory will come from a Domestic Manufacturing Conversion Grant; it’s being restructured in partnership with the U.S. Department of Energy, which initially awarded the funds. And last week during a quarterly earnings call, GM CEO Mary Barra said the company will have hybrids in more than one segment, meaning customers who covet GM-built gas-electric models won’t be limited to three-row SUVs.

Eric Stafford’s automobile addiction began before he could walk, and it has fueled his passion to write news, reviews, and more for Car and Driver since 2016. His aspiration growing up was to become a millionaire with a Jay Leno–like car collection. Apparently, getting rich is harder than social-media influencers make it seem, so he avoided financial success entirely to become an automotive journalist and drive new cars for a living. After earning a journalism degree at Central Michigan University and working at a daily newspaper, the years of basically burning money on failed project cars and lemon-flavored jalopies finally paid off when Car and Driver hired him. His garage currently includes a 2010 Acura RDX, a manual '97 Chevy Camaro Z/28, and a '90 Honda CRX Si.

Netmarble, a studio and publisher known for its free-to-play games on mobile and PC, recently unveiled the launch date for The Seven Deadly Sins: Origin, its next major release that’s very much coming for the Genshin Impact crowd.

The Seven Deadly Sins: Origin is an open-world action RPG based on the world of The Seven Deadly Sins anime and manga. The game takes place in the world of Britannia, which looks similar to the way it does in the anime.

That said, the story of the game is not based on the anime or manga, Instead, it’s an original story set in the universe of the show. You play as Prince Tristan of the Kingdom of Liones who must find a way to bring order back to Britannia.

The gameplay should be familiar to anyone who played a free-to-play action RPG in the last decade. It features action combat, a roster of diverse heroes that you assemble to form teams, and a large pretty world to explore where you’ll also get to meet new characters.

Speaking of which, while Origin has its own original story, expect to be teaming up with characters from the series - some of whom wouldn't be able to meet naturally. You can thank the game's multiversal story for that.

Beyond all the serious business of saving the world and battling monsters, The Seven Deadly Sins: Origin also has a cozy, relaxing side. You’ll be able to fish, cook, forage and take on the beauty of the world with your pals.

The game has a bit of an unusual release schedule. It’s set to arrive on PC, PS5, iOS, and Android. PC (Steam), and PS5 players will be able to jump in starting March 16, whereas the global mobile launch is scheduled for one week later, on March 23.

#4291. Xbox's next-gen console to reportedly cost up to $1,800, with a Helix handheld coming in 2027 and a full release in holiday 2028

The price of Xbox's next generation console, codename Project Helix, may be higher than many of us expected. Helix, which is reportedly going to ship as two devices, is estimated to cost close to $2,000.

That's according to a wide-ranging new report and profile of Xbox CEO Asha Sharma by Fortune Magazine. In it, it's claimed Xbox's internal estimates have arrived at a staggering price: between $1,200 (~£900) and $1,800 (~£1,345). This is not simply the cost of components and manufacturing, it's the price Xbox reportedly expects the console to cost consumers, assuming it is not subsidised.

The report also revealed that Helix, which Sharma previously stressed is a family of devices, will indeed encompass two consoles: a traditional box and a handheld. Xbox is reportedly planning to release the handheld Helix first, with the current target being holiday 2027. The main, under-the-tv box Helix is instead planned for holiday 2028.

As shocking as these prices may be, they're not entirely out of line with what industry insiders have been saying we should expect. The global AI-driven memory and storage shortage has caused prices of practically all consumer electronics to shoot up significantly. The shortages also resulted in Xbox, PlayStation and Nintendo all raising prices of their years-old current-gen consoles on multiple occasions since their launches.

The flagship Xbox Series X, which launched at $500 / £450, now costs $800 / £670. The Series S, initially pitched as a budget-friendly, entry level console now costs $500 / £430, up from $300 / £250. A Helix console at $1,200 would mean a 50 percent increase on top of Series X's current price.

The report also revealed an internal divide between the Xbox and Windows teams over which operating system handheld Helix should run. Microsoft's Windows team reportedly wanted a slimmed down version of Windows Home to run on the handheld, whereas the Xbox team instead wanted specialised software to maximise gaming performance.

The disagreements reportedly got so bad that an Xbox executive threatened to end the handheld project entirely. It's not clear what the big-box Helix is going to run, however, seeing as it was envisioned as a bridge between PC and Xbox capable of playing games from both platforms.

#4290. The AirPods Max 2 Are $120 Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Apple’s latest flagship headphones, the AirPods Max 2, are currently $429 (down from $549.99) on Amazon—taking $120 off every available color and bringing them within $30 of their lowest price ever, according to price trackers. They’re still a considerable investment, but the AirPods Max 2 have plenty going for them, earning a spot on our shopping editor Daniel’s list of the best over-ear headphones of 2026. PCMag is similarly impressed, giving them an “outstanding” rating for their excellent sound quality, noise cancellation, and seamless integration with Apple devices.

Much of what makes the AirPods Max 2 an improvement over their predecessor comes down to two hardware changes: a new high-dynamic-range amplifier and Apple’s H2 chip, replacing the older H1. The amplifier delivers cleaner audio, fuller bass, and better instrument separation with minimal distortion, while spatial audio makes movies and compatible music feel more immersive. Meanwhile, the H2 chip improves active noise cancellation, Transparency mode, and Voice Isolation, which helps callers hear you more clearly when you're speaking from a crowded environment.

Also, noise cancellation is now adaptive, meaning it adjusts how much outside sound comes through as your surroundings change, saving you from constantly switching listening modes. You’ll also find some useful additions beyond audio performance, including Live Translation, which lets you hear supported foreign-language conversations translated through the headphones when paired with a compatible iPhone. The buttons on the headphones can also act as a remote shutter for your iPhone’s camera, which could come in handy for group photos or hands-free recording. All said, the AirPods Max 2 makes the most sense for someone already invested in Apple's ecosystem, since several of their more interesting features depend on compatible Apple devices and software.
