#4289. The Video Games And More You Should Play This Weekend – October 16, 2026

Welcome to spooky month where lawns suddenly grow giant skeletons, it's cold enough for a hoodie, the remakes of Resident Evil 2, 3, and 4 released on Switch 2, and we're all prepping to hunker down for Winter. And Grand Theft Auto VI.

We're still in the thick of new release season which, fun fact, used to just be the month of November. But now it starts in August and ends in March, give or take a few weeks.

Before we get to our regularly scheduled weekend recommendations, though, here are some of the biggest Game Informer stories of the week.

It has been a long wait for a brand new Castlevania game, and frankly, I think Belmont's Curse has been worth the time spent in the queue. Charles Harte agrees, as he wrote in his review, "Belmont's Curse is a strong reminder of why the Castlevania series is so influential, with a rich world, tense combat, and a map chock-full of secrets. While this latest entry certainly pays homage to the Castlevanias of yore, it also breathes fresh life into the franchise, making me excited for its future. Castlevania is back, and between Rose Belmont and Evil Empire, it's in great hands."

The game comes from the team behind Dead Cells, a game heavily influenced by Castlevania to begin with, and the result shows how great a fit the team and franchise is. Controlling Rose Belmont feels great, the world is big and fun to explore without being overbearing, and beating each boss is a rewarding challenge. I often feel one mark of a good game for me is if it keeps me up past my bedtime, and Belmont's Curse is, unfortunately, doing a great job of making me feel tired all day. But that's a good thing.

The short pitch for Silver Pines is that it is a Metroid-inspired game crossed with a Silent Hill game. That's a good hook on its own, but what makes it stand out (at least in the limited time I have so far spent with it), is its animation. I don't know that it was actually rotoscoped (think the original 1989 Prince of Persia), but it feels like it was, which makes it look great and lends it an intentional level of uncanny valley that aids the creepy atmosphere. Throw in a dash of Twin Peaks energy, and you end up with something that feels unique against a sea of comparable (but often well-designed) Metroid-inspired games.

Just Dance is an frequent tradition in my household. We don't play it every year, but I was eager to check out Decades of Hits because of its included soundtrack. Just Dance generally leans on contemporary music and as a dad facing down his forties (who is still very cool, right? Right?!) I appreciate the chance to learn about recent pop hits and make a fool of myself in front of my family, but I like how musically diverse this entry is.

You have Sabrina Carpenter, Chappell Roan, and Olivia Rodrigo songs (which I adore), but you also get ABBA, Spice Girls, No Doubt, Daft Punk, Britney Spears, Sum 41, TLC, Gorillaz, Run DMC, Billy Joel, and Elton John among many others.

Also, in recent years, Just Dance has started its own tradition of including at least one shockingly impressive stop-motion animated sequence. I am not sure what executive at Ubisoft is accidentally approving the budget for the continued creation of these types of videos, but I hope they never notice because they're great.

You really can’t go wrong with any Jackbox game. Sure, there are some occasional duds in the packages, but there are always at least one or two super fun games that are always worth returning to. 12 is out this week and includes a game about sorting trivia questions into the right categories at high speed, one where you try to make the most ridiculous greeting card, a game encouraging silly debates, one that identifies connections among everyone playing, and finally Hyperface, where you manipulate selfies.

After an earlier release on VR platforms, the rhythm game Maestro recently made its way onto Switch and Switch 2, including a physical game cartridge launch. If you've always secretly loved the idea of stepping up onto the podium for a try at the conductor's gig, it's one worth tracking down. The game includes a number of classical hits from the likes of Beethoven, Vivaldi, and Tchaikovsky, but there's also the chance to play more contemporary tracks like those you'd hear in Star Wars or Game of Thrones. With Joy-Cons in hand, you'll keep the tempo, cue in musicians, adjust dynamics, and more. The accompanying visuals and "storytelling" aren't going to blow your mind, but the included music is hard to beat, and with occasional exceptions where the game misread my motions, I found the motion-tracking mostly up to the task of nailing the fantasy.

If you spent hours playing the early Toy Story games, then the retro compilation masters at Digital Eclipse have something for you in the form of Toy Story: Retro Roundup. On top of Toy Story, Toy Story 2: Buzz Lightyear to the Rescue, Toy Story 2, Buzz Lightyear of Star Command, and Toy Story Racer (including multiple versions of many of these), you can also get your hands on the 1998 A Bug's Life game. It may not have the breadth of extra content of something like Digital Eclipse's Atari 50 or Tetris Forever, but it's a fun stroll down memory lane with some behind-the-scenes interviews and materials. Alongside this retro collection, there's also the separate Toy Story 3 Complete Edition, also released by Digital Eclipse this week.

Kingdom Hearts Collection [I~III] – yes, that’s really what it’s called – is out on Nintendo Switch 2, and after spending some time with it, man, it feels right at home on the console. This collection includes Kingdom Hearts HD 1.5 +2.5 ReMix (which itself includes a bunch of Kingdom Hearts games, including 1 and 2), Kingdom Hearts HD 2.8 Final Chapter Prologue, and Kingdom Hearts III + Re Mind DLC. Basically, it has everything you need to catch up on the Kingdom Hearts saga before Kingdom Hearts IV launches next year.

As for actually playing these games, they feel amazing on Switch 2, courtesy of HD resolutions, on handheld and docked, and 60 FPS gameplay. I don’t know why exactly, but playing the Kingdom Hearts series on Switch 2 just feels right. It probably has something to do with just how many of these games were Nintendo console originals. Nonetheless, if you’re new to Kingdom Hearts or looking for a new place to tear up while "Dearly Beloved" plays, this Switch 2 collection will satisfy your needs.

This new standalone version of Forest Shuffle does more than transplant the theming into the Smoky Mountains of North America. It has also allowed its creators to refine balance, add new elements (like an intriguing new water/river terrain), create a more vibrant and visually beautiful presentation, and overall, up the value and playability of the game across the board. In short, it's what I think is the best version of an already very good game.

For newcomers, the Forest Shuffle formula is a card game that involves a mix of card hand management and tableau-building, as you slowly play out a forest scene onto the table, filled with unique trees, animals, and plants, and gathering points for the types and variety of things living in your woods. It's an approachable and visually enchanting game that still offers plenty of strategic depth as you plan out the shape and potential of your nature scene, all while planning against the impending arrival of winter.

#4288. Marathon may actually have a shot as Server Slam hits it big on Steam

Marathon's latest public open weekend has kicked off, and it looks like enough people have showed up to play it.

Things are officially looking better for Marathon. The game’s Server Slam, essentially an open beta that’s freely available everywhere, has kicked off. This is the last chance for anyone curious about Bungie’s next big game to play it before it officially goes on sale next week.

Considering how unpopular the game has been throughout its development, and judging by the feedback from earlier closed beta tests, the turnout for the Server Slam is definitely encouraging.

The Marathon Server Slam has been online for a little over 12 hours on Steam, PS5, and Xbox Series X/S. Only one of those platforms shares player numbers, of course, but it's enough to offer some visibility into how popular the test has been.

Immediately after servers went live, Marathon shot up the Steam most played charts. The game quickly achieved a peak of 143,621 concurrent players (via SteamDB), which is very respectable. Given that the Server Slam will be available for the entire weekend, there’s every chance the numbers will go up.

These figures are particularly encouraging when you look at Arc Raiders, the current top dog in the extraction shooter space, and the game that stole Marathon’s thunder last year when the two of them were being promoted by their publishers. Marathon, of course, got delayed out of 2025, which allowed Arc Raiders to launch first. It, of course, ended up proving that extraction shooters can be mainstream hits.

Like Marathon, Arc Raiders hosted its own server slam just a few days before its launch. That open test attracted 189,668 concurrent players at its peak, which is not that far off from Marathon’s so far.

Assuming positive word of mouth continues, more players are bound to jump in over the weekend and perhaps get Marathon to match or even exceed Arc Raiders’ record. At any rate, this is good news for beleaguered Bungie and points to what could be a big launch.

#4286. Scientists discover a hidden immune weapon against deadly lung cancer

Scientists have identified a previously overlooked type of immune cell that can detect and destroy small cell lung cancer, the most aggressive form of lung cancer worldwide.

The discovery, reported in the journal Cancer Cell by researchers at WEHI, revealed that patients with greater numbers of these specialized immune cells tended to survive longer. The findings point to a previously unrecognized immune defense that could provide a new way to attack tumors that are notoriously difficult to treat.

The research team also demonstrated that medications already in use could enhance the ability of these immune cells to kill cancer. This approach could eventually improve existing treatments and increase the number of patients who benefit from immunotherapy.

Why Small Cell Lung Cancer Is So Difficult to Treat

Small cell lung cancer (SCLC) is among the deadliest cancers, with fewer than 7% of patients surviving for five years after diagnosis.

Immunotherapy has dramatically improved treatment for several types of cancer, but its success against SCLC has been much more limited. Only a small proportion of patients experience lasting benefits.

A major challenge is the cancer's ability to escape immune surveillance. SCLC tumors frequently avoid detection by the conventional immune cells that many immunotherapy treatments depend on, allowing the disease to persist despite the body's natural defenses.

The findings published in Cancer Cell suggest there may be another way for the immune system to recognize and attack these tumors. By identifying a previously overlooked immune pathway, researchers have uncovered a potential strategy for overcoming the cancer's ability to remain hidden.

Professor Kate Sutherland, the study's senior author and a laboratory head at WEHI, said the discovery could help address a longstanding challenge in lung cancer research: finding ways to make immunotherapy effective for more patients.

"For decades, we've known that small cell lung cancer is exceptionally good at hiding from the immune system," Prof Sutherland said.

"We've found an immune cell that appears to be able to see through that disguise.

"Strikingly, patients whose tumors contained more of these specialized immune cells often had better treatment outcomes.

"This is the strongest evidence yet that gamma delta T cells are present in small cell lung cancers and appear capable of fighting the disease.

"It suggests these cells may offer an alternative immune attack route for tumors that are able to evade conventional cancer-fighting immune cells, opening up new possibilities for treating a cancer that has seen few advances over the past decades."

Miniature Tumors Reveal a Powerful Immune Response

To investigate whether gamma delta T cells could directly attack lung cancer, the scientists developed miniature tumors in the laboratory using tissue samples donated by patients. The samples were obtained through the Victorian Cancer Biobank in partnership with the Royal Melbourne Hospital.

These miniature tumors, often called "mini tumors," provide one of the most realistic laboratory models of SCLC created so far, allowing researchers to study how cancer behaves and responds to potential treatments.

The team then introduced gamma delta T cells collected from the same patients' blood into the tumor models. The results showed that these immune cells were highly effective at identifying and destroying SCLC cells.

The scientists went a step further by testing whether two medications already approved for other uses could make this immune response even stronger. The drugs were tarlatamab (a cancer treatment approved for SCLC) and zoledronate (approved for osteoporosis and cancer-related bone disease).

Each medication contributed in a different way. Zoledronate increased the number of available gamma delta T cells and improved their ability to identify the lung cancer tumors. Tarlatamab helped bring the immune cells and cancer cells together, effectively directing the immune attack toward the tumors.

When used together, the drugs substantially increased the ability of gamma delta T cells to kill cancer cells in laboratory experiments. The findings suggest that combining existing treatments could provide a new strategy for strengthening the immune system's response to SCLC.

Could Existing Drugs Lead to New Lung Cancer Therapies?

Dr. Jin Ng, the study's first author, said the discovery could also support a treatment approach known as adoptive cell transfer. This involves collecting immune cells, multiplying them outside the body, and then delivering large numbers of those cells back into a patient to help fight cancer.

Because methods for expanding and reinfusing gamma delta T cells have already been developed, the researchers believe their findings could help accelerate the development of new treatments.

"For patients with lower gamma delta T cell levels, therapies such as zoledronate may offer a way to boost their cancer-fighting immune cells and responsiveness to treatments," Dr. Ng said.

"As therapies that expand and reinfuse gamma delta T cells already exist, our findings raise the exciting possibility of rapidly translating this discovery into new treatment strategies.

"This could open an entirely new avenue for personalized treatment approaches in small cell lung cancer and other solid cancers that are often missed by conventional immune cells."

Clues From Major Lung Cancer Immunotherapy Trials

The investigation began with an examination of data from two influential clinical trials, IMpower133 and CASPIAN. Both studies helped establish immunotherapy as a standard treatment option for patients with small cell lung cancer.

Researchers noticed evidence pointing toward an important role for gamma delta T cells and used sophisticated genomic and computational techniques to investigate further.

Analyses conducted by WEHI's genomics and bioinformatics teams revealed that patients with stronger gamma delta T cell signatures were among those who benefited most from immunotherapy.

Together with the laboratory findings, these results strengthen the evidence that gamma delta T cells play an important role in the immune response to SCLC and could become useful targets for future therapies.

The WEHI team is now working with scientists at the Doherty Institute to build on the discovery, with the goal of translating the findings into a new class of immunotherapy drugs for small cell lung cancer.

The research received support from Cancer Council Victoria, the National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC), The CASS Foundation, and The International Association for the Study of Lung Cancer.

Materials provided by Walter and Eliza Hall Institute. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4284. Even at their most chaotic and conflicted, BioWare were the heart of the western computer RPG

Life... is strength. The gears of this world are tuned for a quick death. Doomity-doom-doom! We is all doooooo-oooomed! The Guardian says for me to be cheerful, and to not repeat what he says all the... oh. I'm Commander Shepard, and this is my favourite store on the Citadel. You simians may merely refer to me as 'Sir,' if you prefer a less... syllable-intensive workout. I have seen the throne of the Gods, and it was empty. Stand in the ashes of a trillion dead souls and ask the ghosts if honour matters. There are people like you everywhere. It's just that not everyone has the words for it yet. You exist because we allow it, and you will end because we demand it. Let's call that finished. You must gather your party before venturing forth.

I'm not even all that much of a BioWare enthusiast. I'm not sure I'm in mourning, exactly. Partly that's because videogame studios are big corporate abstractions, unworthy of our silly human emotions; the story here is that a bunch of people have lost their livelihoods, after their employers triumphantly thrust themselves into unbelievable debt. And partly it's because, as with any studio of over 30 years standing, BioWare was a much-caulked and jury-rigged ship of Theseus – paddling around in the doldrums of 2026, I'm not entirely sure what I should be mourning, any more.

Reportedly, there were around 50 staff left at BioWare, mostly labouring away on Mass Effect 5, when EA closed them this week. A lot of those who worked on the studio's formative games, including the very first Baldur's Gate, are long departed. The past decade especially has seen a steady exodus of BioWare talent, alongside a barrage of "veteran" start-ups and spiritual successor projects, many of which have sadly now faded and folded.

Within that arc, my own appreciation for BioWare has waxed and waned. I honestly can't remember enough of Baldur's Gate 2 to have strong opinions, though I overall acknowledge its brilliance. I probably felt closest to the studio during the heyday of the original Mass Effect trilogy. The original's Vigil scene still gives me shivers, and who could forget the prodigious act of fan service demolition that was Mass Effect 2's opening, to say nothing of its legendary "Suicide Mission" (I left Mordin behind).

I will always carry a candle for Dragon Age: Inquisition – a generous and unwieldy return to save-the-world-dom after Dragon Age 2's leaner city story, which I cherish for its beautiful Tarot art, its bustling hub castle, and its spread of rambunctious and difficult companion characters. I greatly enjoy Ferelden as a setting, all the different yarns the various races spin about the world's becoming. Still, I bounced right off Dragon Age: The Veilguard's on-the-nose, corridor-like design, despite Nic's guarded exhortations.

Anthem? I was keener on that than a lot of you, but I remember it mostly as a conflicted yet promising aviator with nowhere to go – so much BioWare flourish, floundering through a swamp of live service. I thought Mass Effect: Andromeda was a wide, shallow bowl of lukewarm space-gruel lent relish only by its launch bugs. There are other BioWare games I simply haven't played – Neverwinter Nights (well, apart from the five minutes I managed, before it became clear my computer at the time really couldn't run it) and its celebrated modding toolkit, the Orientalised fable Jade Empire and, whisper it, almighty KOTOR.

As for my experience of BioWare as a games journalist, it's pretty brief and polarised. On the one hand, spending three hours with a preview build of Inquisition in a London warehouse, alongside such guiding lights as former PCGamer scribe Chris Thursten, is probably as close to heaven as I'll get before my inevitable deathbed recantation. On the other, there was that humiliating interview with co-founder Dr Ray Muzyka, when I was terrified our mic wouldn't record him properly. I resorted to kneeling between Muzyka and the actual interviewer, jerking the dictaphone back and forth in my desperation to guzzle up every last drop of Muzyka wisdom. Not even Khalisah al-Jilani was this cringe. I haven't been allowed near a BioWare executive since.

The thing is, you don't have to have enjoyed, or even played many BioWare games to understand the difference they've made, and why the newly private EA's closure of the studio is such a kick in the guts, however presaged by years of interference, mismanagement, and neglect. As regards North American and European sci-fantasy incarnations, at least, BioWare have always been the RPG's sleeping sentinel. They wrote the genre around themselves, producing and revising a vocabulary over decades that so many other designers, writers and modders are in conversation with – whether courting comparison, or critiquing and even despising.

BioWare are why D&D RPGs took off. They're the creators of by far the most influential adaptation of the tabletop game's forbidding ruleset. They are the studio you probably think of, when you think of party-based stories with branching plots. They are the blockbuster joint you think of, rivalled only by Final Fantasy and more recently Larian, when you think of companions who deserve the starring role.

Even their stumbles have thrown open ground for other writers, designers and artists to investigate: that main-and-supporting-band antagonism between critical path and sidequest, for example - between yet another chronicle of destined heroes averting some great evil, and the huggable humour of playing go-between for a knight who's too shy to hit on somebody. How to reconcile the grandiosity with the closeness is the trad fantasy RPG's founding dilemma. They popularised in-game romances and, yes, sex scenes that generally feel like the convincing result of hours of careful narrative distillation, and occasionally like a helping of cheesecake unlocked by pawing away at a hidden skilltree.

They are the studio who got us tallying up colour-coded Good and Bad points to shape both protagonist and endgame - so tacky, but undeniably striking as a vehicle for stark shifts and flavourful writing, as demonstrated in reverse by the omnivorous wishy-washiness of Mass Effect: Andromeda. Their morality systems are a place from which to start thinking about other, less videogamey, more organic and conflicting entanglements of Morality and System. They introduced a particular species of videogame celebrity with Commander Shepard, and they presided over a very particular stage in the evolving friction between creators and "audience" with Mass Effect 3's much-revised ending. While I'd made up my mind to stop covering EA games, following the Saudi-led acquisition, I was curious, if not optimistic, to see what they might have done with Mass Effect 5.

Above all, BioWare were the wizards of words. They had less-good scripts but I don't think they ever penned a bad one. Looking back over their games is dizzying and deafening, like opening a door in a dark, crumbling house to reveal a crowd of revellers - demons and courtesans and krogans and fops and berserkers, painted glorious shades by secret firelight and dancing in a ring.

So many words! How many words? Tens of millions, surely. Words that conjure words, raising them from the dust: Go for the optics, Chatika! Go for the eyes, Boo! Words seemingly drawn from some personal well of pain and tenderness, easily reduced to authorial self-commentary, that stick in my mind where so much else about the ageing fantasy has lost its charm. Here is Aveline, the aforesaid timid knight, to play us out. "That big man made every step of the story my choice. I loved that. He died of the Wasting in a Denerim ward... those last weeks, I read to him. I had to take his hand to turn the pages, and I couldn't tell if he was too weak, or if it was the old game. He smiled at that, his big girl… Don't let anyone tell you how to mourn. And when someone says 'move on', you take their hand and say, 'my choice.'"

Android, Mac, Nintendo Switch, PC, Xbox, iOS

It started with Crash Bandicoot but ultimately ended with Dark Cloud. When I first picked up a PS2 controller more than 20 years ago, it was to spin and jump as a shorts-bearing marsupial. Whenever I return to the system in my 30s it's to roam the dungeons of Level-5's village-builder RPG. And yet, even with an Asus ROG Ally kitted out for PS2 emulation I still sometimes struggle to put down the DualSense or close the gaming laptop and return to the simpler days of Nolun (or Norune) Village.

Pairing one of the best gaming phones with one of the best mobile controllers has half-solved this blindspot already. With the GameSir Pocket Taco (currently on sale for $27.99 at Amazon) at my side I've been blasting through GameBoy Advance titles. Being able to whip out my phone at a moment's notice and tap through gaming history rather than doomscroll has been a backlog-saver.

It's something I would rarely do at home, not when I have kit to test on the very latest releases. While it demands the right game (and what could be better than quickly hopping into a world-building Dark Cloud dungeon run while waiting for the bus), being able to jump in and out of classic titles while on the go has turbo-charged my GBA adventures. It doesn't, however, feature the sticks required for PlayStation adventures. Instead, I'm hoping the Abyxlute M5 will do the same for my PS2 memories.

The magnetic attachment and slide-up design of the new Abxylute M5 and M6 mean they could, theoretically, stay permanently attached to your phone. Push up that screen and you've got a full selection of controls waiting for you; two thumbsticks (low-profile carbon-film on the M5 and capacitive on the M6), d-pad, face buttons, shoulders and triggers (Hall effect no less, on the M6). In other words, a full set of PS2 controls ready and waiting whenever I find myself reaching for some thought-preventing entertainment.

The Abxylute pop-up mobile controllers are currently still in their Kickstarter phase, though Early Bird pricing has now ended. The brand does have some presence at wider retailers, like Amazon, so hopefully we'll see these gamepads popping up more across the web shortly.

While we wait for the M5 and M6 to return to the shelves, the Abxylute M4 is 10% off at Amazon. It's a much smaller design, but still manages to cram two thumbsticks and both L1/2 and R1/2 bumpers.

It's those two sticks that are driving the M5 and M6 to the top of my brain. Yes, yes, I know. Most of the modern mobile controllers worth their salt will come with a full set of controls. Taking a look at the top contenders across the market, though, they're all designed to accompany your phone rather than staying permanently attached.

The ELO Vagabond will need its own pouch in my backpack and you can forget about keeping the Razer Kishi V3 Pro in a back pocket. Even the foldable Asus ROG Tessen (while taking up less space) will likely be left at home when leaving in a rush. Hell, even Razer's tiny Prio controller runs the risk of being stuck in a drawer.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

Often, the best controller is the one you have with you - and that's doubly true of mobile gaming. Abxylute's gamepads will have to prove that they're light, thin, and sturdy enough to withstand days in pockets, but their permanent connection to my phone might finally bring my PS2 backlog under control. Who knows, I might even get round to ripping my Dark Cloud 2 disc.

#4282. Resident Evil: Requiem made more money from PlayStation discs than on Xbox in total, reportedly, as Sony's march to a discless future continues

In Japan, physical sales are even stronger.

Physical disc-based copies of Resident Evil: Requiem on PlayStation consoles may have been more popular than all versions of the game on Xbox, a new report has found, while unearthing a trove of other statistics about physical sales.

The information comes from Alinea Analytics analyst Rhys Elliott, who published a newsletter breaking down the top PlayStation games by physical copies sold. According to Elliott's estimates, Resident Evil: Requiem sold 1.3 million physical copies, which makes up 15 percent of the game's total revenue, and 30.2 percent of its revenue on PlayStation. Physical copies made 1.8x more revenue on PlayStation than it made from all copies sold - digital and physical - on Xbox, the newsletter reports.

Meanwhile, physical sales of 007 First Light, Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced, and Crimson Desert are all under 30 percent of their relevant totals, according to this report. This makes physical sales a minority, but if the figures are accurate, proves that they still account for a hearty chunk of the total revenue for these single-player games. Wolverine, another example used by Rhys, seems to owe 800,000 of its 2.6m sales to physical copies, as well as nearly a third of its total revenue.

In Japan, there's even stronger support for physical game sales. First-party PlayStation and Japan-centric games such as Capcom releases tend to sell more physical copies there. have a larger slice of its total sales be physical than other types of games.

Elliott surmises that's because physical games are more "ingrained in the culture" there. 44 percent of Pragmata's copies sold were physical, for example, and 44.1 percent of Onimusha: Way of the Sword's sales were physical too. With Japan responsible for 12 percent of Onimusha's sales and 11 percent of Pragmata's, the nation's buying habits are enough to make a substantial impact on global statistics.

It's against that backdrop that the new, digital-only and Japan-exclusive PS5 Black has launched in Japan, for $350, with a separate disc drive sold for $75. Elliott interprets this as an attempt to "nudge" Japanese users towards buying games digitally.

However, whereas physical sales for single-player games seem healthy, it's a different situation for sports games. PlayStation's current top-selling game - FC 27 - owes only 8.9 percent of its sales to physical. Still, as is noted by Elliott, the relative popularity of physical games on PlayStation is likely a key component of the backlash Sony has faced with its decision to shift towards digital.

Only Monday, PlayStation founder Ken Kutaragi said that discs became a "metaphor for 'owning' games", and that the upset at their discontinuation was probably a "mentality issue". Sony has also pledged to continue its plans to move away from discs despite the associated outrage.

#4281. 10 Hacks Every YouTube TV User Should Know

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

YouTube TV is one of several streaming services for live TV—and a particularly good pick for sports fans who want to be able to watch and record every matchup from their favorite leagues. If YouTube TV is your platform of choice, here are the tips and tricks to optimize your experience.

If you and members of your household have wildly different streaming tastes, you can create unique profiles for each person to prevent recommendations from one from influencing another and to separate DVR libraries. A single subscription can be shared with five additional family members, who technically must reside in the same household, and those who are outside of the home need to check in once every three months (more on that below) to maintain access. You're also limited to three active streams at a time, so six people can't be watching on six separate devices at once.

If you travel frequently or are part of family sharing based anywhere besides your home, make sure you launch YouTube TV whenever you are in the account's home area. YouTube TV requires all users to check in every 90 days to avoid losing access when away from your home area. (For baseball fans, MLB imposes viewing restrictions after just 30 days.) If you get in the habit of resetting your 90-day window, you're less likely to find yourself locked out when you're on the road.

YouTube TV has pre-built multiviews that show you three or four channels at once—these are often either several games across a single sport or a combination of sports channels. Earlier this year, the platform launched customizable multiview, which allows you to select up to four channels across content categories. This means you can choose the specific games you want or mix and match sports with news, TV shows, and movies so that everyone in your household can be entertained. From any livestream, press the down button and select Multiview > Your multiview to choose up to four channels.

YouTube TV is heavy on features benefiting sports fans. If you can't begin a broadcast when a game starts, the platform offers you several options when you launch the stream: Start from the beginning, join live, or catch up to live through key plays. The latter shows you only the highlights of the action then drops you into the live stream, so you don't miss anything important but don't have to watch or fast-forward through the entire game to see what's happening now. You can also find the key plays view via Scores & stats at the bottom of the screen. This feature is supported for events from the NFL, NBA, MLB, NHL, college football, and college basketball.

Another way to avoid spoilers for live sports is to hide scores, which prevents final scores or live previews from launching before you start watching. For NFL, NBA, and MLB games, you can hide scores on a team-by-team or league-wide basis. If a league doesn't have pages for individual teams, you can hide scores for the whole league. On the team or league page, go to More and turn on Hide all scores for this team/league.

If you aren't sure what you want to watch on YouTube TV and none of the recommendations do it for you, you can use the platform's Live Guide just like the channel guide from traditional cable. However, this version is customizable, so you don't have to scroll through dozens of channels you never watch to reach the ones you do. On the web browser or app, go to Settings > Live Guide to reorder, favorite, or hide channels from your list. This can't be done directly from a streaming box, but when you open the live guide on your TV, select the Custom filter view to see your updates.

Your watch history informs recommendations on YouTube and YouTube TV, which is useful for discovering new content but also means that your algorithm can be influenced by anything and everything you watch. If you don't want certain content affecting what you're served—let's say you're binge-watching a show you wouldn't normally or you have someone staying at your house and using your account—you can pause your watch history temporarily. Tap your profile photo, go to Settings > Privacy, and toggle on Pause watch history.

YouTube TV has unlimited DVR, but that doesn't mean you need to fill your storage with content you don't plan to watch. You can record everything from an entire sport or league, or you can select only the specific teams or franchises you care about. This depends on how you set up recording: You can search for the league and tap Add + or the "+" icon to get every game, or search for your favorite team or individual matchup. DVR playback has similar options to live events you start on delay, including resuming where you left off and watching key plays.

Some content you record from the live feed may also be available as video on demand (VOD). With the former, you can fast-forward through ads; with the latter, ads can't be skipped. However, you may be able to choose your version if you used DVR. Click down on the stream to see whether you're on VOD or DVR, then go back to the show page and select Choose a Version > DVR (if available).

If you have technical issues with YouTube TV, the built-in Stats for Nerds feature can help you diagnose the problem (and whether it's coming from your network or YouTube TV itself). For example, if your resolution drops significantly, connection speed, buffer health, and dropped frames can give you a clue as to whether your wifi is the culprit. Access Stats for Nerds on your TV via player controls > More > Bug icon. On your computer, right-click on the video player; on mobile, tap More > Stats from the video player.

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City.

#4279. The Sims 4 is getting paid mods, and a new in-game currency to pay for them

The Sims 4 is about to launch a couple of major initiatives that may not exactly be what players have been looking for.

The Sims 4 has officially entered into its paid mods era. Developer Maxis announced The Sims Maker Program, and The Sims 4 Marketplace - two new destinations to publish, and discover new add-on content.

The two new features will arrive first on PC and Mac, starting March 17. PlayStation and Xbox players will receive them over the next couple of months, which is very much in line with how other franchises have rolled out their own paid mods systems.

The Maker Program is a way for community creators to get their work out there, and profit from it. The content they create will end up on the Marketplace, where other players can browse it, and pay for it if they want to use it in their game.

Created content will be available across all platforms. All previous packs The Sims 4 players should be familiar with will move to the Marketplace, too: once the feature launches, this is where you’re going to find Expansion Packs, Game Packs, Stuff Packs, Kits, and other official additions created by Maxis.

Alongside this, the game is also introducing a new virtual currency - Moola - which is what you’re going to use to purchase Maker Packs, as well as Sims 4 Kits in the Marketplace. A portion of your purchase will go to the creator (roughly 30%) of what you buy.

It is worth noting, however, that you’ll still be able to buy Expansion Packs, Game Packs, and Stuff Packs without having to purchase Moola, or go through the Marketplace. Kits are the only items that will no longer be available on the existing PC/console storefronts. Moola comes in the usual denominations you’ve seen a hundred items in other games’ in-game shops.

In order to start creating custom content and publishing it on the Marketplace, you’ll need to first be accepted into the program. Once that’s done, you’ll be able to directly publish your content on the Marketplace.

In the blog post announcing the new features, Maxis said that creators will have control over the price of their content, and how it's packaged. These are referred to as Maker Packs, which can include Create-a-Sim or Build/Buy items.

You can apply to become a Maker from March 5. Once accepted, you’ll also get access to the Maker Suite, which is what you’re going to use to test your work to ensure it runs smoothly on the different platforms, and eventually publish it.

#4275. Scientists discover Arctic sea ice loss could be far worse than predicted

The Arctic could lose roughly one million square kilometers more winter sea ice than scientists previously predicted, according to new research that draws on evidence from a warmer Arctic 6,000 years ago. By using ancient climate records to refine modern computer models, researchers have produced more reliable estimates of how much ice may disappear in the coming decades.

The findings, published in Nature Geoscience, could also have important consequences for winter weather in the United Kingdom and other parts of Europe.

Much of the attention surrounding Arctic ice loss has focused on the dramatic retreat of summer sea ice. Winter ice, however, has received considerably less scrutiny, even though climate models show their greatest disagreements when predicting how much ice will cover the Arctic during the colder months.

Why Arctic Winter Sea Ice Matters for Global Weather

Winter sea ice influences weather far beyond the Arctic by controlling how much heat and moisture escape from the ocean into the atmosphere. Declining ice cover has been associated with colder winters and increased snowfall in the United Kingdom, northern Europe, and North America.

Recent observations have also highlighted the Arctic's changing conditions. In March 2025, winter sea ice reached the lowest annual maximum extent recorded since modern monitoring began. The Arctic matched that record again in 2026.

To improve predictions of future ice loss, scientists at the University of Cambridge and Mexico's Centro de Investigación en Matematicas (CIMAT) turned to evidence preserved beneath the Arctic Ocean.

The team assembled the largest database of its kind containing a chemical marker produced by algae that live within sea ice. The evidence came from sediment cores deposited during the mid-Holocene, approximately 6,000 years ago, when small changes in Earth's orbit helped create a warmer Arctic with less extensive ice cover.

These ancient conditions offered a valuable opportunity to investigate how Arctic sea ice responds to warming and to test whether today's climate models accurately represent that response.

Ancient Ocean Sediments Reveal What Climate Models Missed

The researchers examined concentrations of a biomarker known as IP25, which is produced by algae associated with sea ice. They combined these measurements with other chemical markers left behind by organisms living in open water, allowing them to reconstruct Arctic ice conditions thousands of years in the past.

Next, they compared those reconstructions with simulations generated by 27 climate models for the same ancient period.

Using a statistical method called Bayesian weighting, the researchers assigned greater importance to models that more closely matched the historical evidence. Models that performed less accurately received less influence over the final predictions.

Because the underlying physical processes controlling sea ice remain consistent across different climate conditions, the team could apply these same weights to simulations of both the recent past and the future.

The comparison revealed a significant problem: collectively, the climate models predicted more winter sea ice than the ancient evidence supported.

"When we apply the model weights to historical simulations, we completely eliminated the positive bias," said lead author Crystal Fu, from Cambridge's Scott Polar Research Institute. "The future projections similarly end up being lower than previous estimates, by about a million square kilometers."

More Accurate Arctic Ice Forecasts Reduce Uncertainty by 85%

Scientists have collected substantial evidence about Earth's ancient climates, but translating that information into reliable forecasts remains difficult. Both historical reconstructions and future climate simulations contain uncertainties that complicate direct comparisons.

"The scientific community has amassed lots of paleoclimate records over recent decades, but it's remained a challenge to directly link these records to future projections, since there is uncertainty at both ends," said Fu.

The team's new approach substantially narrowed those uncertainties, reducing the range of possible outcomes in some projections by as much as 85%.

"By using evidence from the past, we can now produce more reliable maps of where Arctic winter sea ice is likely to disappear," said co-author Marco Aquino-López, of CIMAT. "These give us a much clearer picture of what the Arctic might look like in the coming decades, and how much uncertainty remains."

Although the warming that occurred during the mid-Holocene was driven by changes in Earth's orbit rather than the human activities responsible for today's warming, Fu emphasized that the response of sea ice to rising temperatures follows the same basic physical principles.

"It doesn't matter if the warming is natural variation or caused by human activity: if it warms, sea ice is going to melt," she said.

Arctic Ice Loss Could Have Wider Consequences for Europe

The findings may have implications that extend beyond the disappearance of Arctic sea ice itself.

When the researchers applied their improved model weighting to projections of precipitation, they found indications that current climate models may also underestimate future snowfall in southeastern Greenland and rainfall across the Mediterranean region.

Greater snowfall over Greenland could affect the stability of the Greenland ice sheet, while increased Mediterranean rainfall could influence future flooding risks in southern Europe.

These potential effects suggest that the consequences of a warming Arctic may be more extensive than existing predictions indicate. The revised simulations offer researchers a clearer foundation for investigating those risks and understanding how changing Arctic conditions could influence weather elsewhere.

The research received partial funding from the US National Science Foundation and the Cambridge Trust. Crystal Fu is a Member of Girton College, Cambridge.

Materials provided by University of Cambridge. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4274. I Tried Google's New Gemini 4 Argon Model, and Here's How It Measures up to GPT-6

David Nield is a technology journalist from Manchester in the U.K. who has been writing about gadgets and apps for more than 20 years.

He has a bachelor's degree in English Literature from Durham University, where he also spent a term as editor of the award-winning student newspaper Palatinate. His journalism career started in print media, where he contributed to and edited several technology magazines and bookazines sold in the U.K. and internationally. More recently, he has worked as a freelancer for some of the biggest technology publications on the web, covering everything from on-the-ground reporting about product launches, to detailed explainers and how-to guides on apps, gadgets, and platforms. His expertise covers broad areas of consumer tech, including smartphones, laptops, wearables, and AI.

Gemini 3.1 Pro launched back in February of this year—quite a while ago, in AI terms. Now Google has jumped to the next full number with Gemini 4 Argon, which it says "excels at deep work in complex, real-world tasks."

You can get the new AI model now inside the Gemini app if you're on the AI Pro ($19.99 a month) or AI Ultra ($99.99 or $199.99 a month) plans. Users on the free and AI Plus ($4.99 a month) plans miss out for now, though you can expect a smaller, cut-down version of Gemini 4 Argon to roll out to these tiers eventually.

Google has a long list of areas where the new AI model excels. There's mention of complex engineering tasks (like checking millions of lines of code for efficiency improvements), predicting molecular structures in chemistry, and delivering 3D visualizations. It also enhances knowledge work across legal, finance, science, and coding.

Google says Gemini 4 Argon is now better at advanced physics modeling, picking through genetic databases, protecting software from vulnerabilities, and resolving problems in physical space (handy for robotics development). The new model "answers questions more directly with less hedging, doesn't repeat itself more than necessary, and skips clichés throughout the responses," according to Google.

With many in the industry worried about an AI-driven apocalypse of some form or another, Google has upped its defenses against misuse and jailbreaking. The company has significantly upgraded its blockers for harmful requests, limits on AI agent overreach, and resistance to malicious prompt injections.

All of these stats are things you would expect to read about in a new AI model announcement, whether it's from Google, Anthropic, or OpenAI: "Our new model is better and safer than ever, and particularly good in these specific benchmarks we've chosen." But is it any more helpful for the average user tapping away in the Gemini app?

I don't have any pressing scientific breakthroughs to research or large-scale software optimizations to run, but I have put Gemini 4 Argon to the test against GPT-6 Sol, which is the latest model available to consumers through the ChatGPT app—though you need to be on at least the Plus plan ($19.99 a month) to get to it.

Make no mistake: Both of these new AI models are quite capable. I first prompted Gemini 4 Argon and GPT-6 Sol with the following: "Using information online, create a web app I can run locally that plots the lighthouses of the world on a map. Clicking on a lighthouse should give me some details about it, show how far its light reaches, and display a simple 2D model showing its shape and height (if known). Put a lighthouse keeper in each model pop-up for scale."

At the highest, slowest levels of "thinking," these AIs took a while to get back to me: Nearly 10 minutes for Gemini and almost 26 minutes for ChatGPT. Both hit the same problem—restricted access to map data online—but both were able to make a rudimentary world map of their own when prompted a second time.

The ChatGPT effort, dubbed "Beacon," wasn't bad, apart from a bit of map clunkiness (it clearly needs to work on its cartography skills). It presented plenty of information, laid out everything well, and gave me nice, neat diagrams on each of the lighthouses featured. The day/night mode worked fine, and it was an impressive bit of coding work, even if it did take 26 minutes.

However, the Gemini effort, called "Pharos," was better in almost every respect. The map was more detailed, lighthouses actually showed their light range (with an impressive animation), and there was more information on each location—perhaps due to Google's expertise in web search. Some of the layout choices were a bit off, but Gemini 4 Argon produced what was clearly the superior app in less than half the time.

Next, I fed PDFs of 10 recent scientific studies into each AI model. The prompt: "Give me a brief summary of each of these studies in plain English, with a single sentence each for the main finding, one study strength, one study weakness, and the main implication." While I hadn't read the studies through entirely myself, I had read news stories on all of them, and I lowered the "thinking" level to low.

This time ChatGPT was faster—25 seconds versus Gemini's 55 seconds—and gave what I thought were the better summaries, at least for my purposes. Gemini did well, and its answers were accurate and to the point, but the summaries ChatGPT gave were easier to understand and shorter. ChatGPT also offered some overall thoughts on the most promising study, which Gemini didn't.

Finally, I checked the imagination and creative juices of the models: "With the success of the Apple TV shows Severance, Slow Horses, Silo, and Widow's Bay, give me five pitches for new show ideas on the platform that combine elements from some or all of these shows. Try and be as original as possible and keep each pitch to around 100 words." I put the "thinking" level back up to "high" for this job.

(Is this how Apple TV executives actually get their ideas nowadays? I hope not.) As far as the answers the AIs gave me, Gemini was faster than ChatGPT (1 minute and 28 seconds against 3 minutes and 48 seconds, respectively), and both served up responses that were a bit more literal in their mashing up than I wanted: failed secret service agents and supernatural terrors abounded, which is I suppose what I asked for.

The ideas were mostly "Widow's Bay set somewhere else" or "Slow Horses set somewhere else," and didn't make a whole lot of sense for the most part, but the best Gemini offering was about a Silo-style underground bunker whose operators go through a Severance-style procedure every 24 hours: outside, the bunker protecting it from zombies by night; inside, the bunker planning social activities for residents by day. It's not perfect, but I might watch the trailer at least.

My favorite of the ChatGPT suggestions was a show about a remote, Severance-style weather station whose employees discover they're not recording the weather—they're creating it. Again, it didn't really logically hold up, but overall I found the suggestions from GPT-6 more nuanced, more likely to be an actual show, and better "thought" through. Maybe Gemini should've given itself more time to "think."

This is only a narrow set of quick tests, but, in my view, it seems Gemini is back to, at the very least, an even playing field with ChatGPT. It performed much better on the lighthouse task, though its summarizing and TV show pitching weren't quite as impressive. Access to the best models requires a subscription on both platforms, but if you're able to, it's worth comparing them against each other on the jobs you most commonly need AI for.

#4272. Nintendo Switch Sports Resort Review - Still In Motion

Originating as a tech demo for the Wii’s motion controls, Nintendo’s Sports series has always found novel and intuitive ways to emulate various athletic endeavors with motion-based video game controllers. Like its predecessors, Switch Sports Resort is a compilation of sports-based minigames built around approachability. Though rightfully known for its enticing multiplayer options, Switch Sports Resort adds new single-player objectives and modes to offer a well-rounded experience for all player counts, making it perhaps the strongest entry in the series to date.

Switch Sports Resort delivers 12 main events to choose from, each with distinct controls, strategies, and rules. Mainstays like bowling, archery, boxing, and golf were my initial destinations to attempt to relive old glories, but newcomers like thumb wrestling, skateboarding, power cruising, and prop plane made me glad for their inclusion.

Skateboarding, power cruising, and prop plane form a new category called leisure sports, which allow for timed exploration of the newly renovated Wuhu Island by land, sea, and sky, respectively. I spent several sessions seeking out landmarks and collecting Spocco Points, the in-game currency used for character customization. And since all the sports’ controls are so intuitive and newcomer-friendly, it was a breeze to jump between activities.

Though they all boil down to moving your arms in something approximating motions you’d make playing the real sport, Nintendo has smartly implemented more variety in its control schemes, all while keeping them easy to pick up. Before the tutorial even explains how to hold the Joy-Con, you can likely accurately guess what the grip and motion will be; even more involved control schemes like basketball and volleyball are easy to remember.

Skateboarding is particularly innovative, as it uses Switch 2’s Joy-Con’s mouse mode to emulate the kick-push of a skater. Meanwhile, prop plane has you holding your Joy-Con like a paper airplane as you fly around blasting balloons and visiting landmarks. However, holding the Joy-Con that way for several minutes inevitably cramped my hand. And though boxing remains one of my favorite sports in the bundle, I needed to retrain my brain, as throwing a punch the correct way doesn’t work with how the game wants you to do it; if I turn my wrist when punching, my character sways instead of attacking.

Playing with friends, both online and in-person, remains the highlight of Switch Sports Resort. The online friend lobby system receives a boost in the form of allowing different sports to take place simultaneously within the same lobby, and when you add in special variants like simultaneous archery, 100-pin bowling, skateboard racing, and prop plane balloon battles, you have a lot of fun options with which to jump in.

Whenever I play solo, the Island Crown is where I spend most of my time. Here, you climb the ranks across nine sports, sometimes with special rulesets, vanquishing NPC opponents until you are the champion. Whether it was taking to increasingly outlandish bowling lanes, windier archery ranges, or simply facing off against more strategic table tennis players, I relished in improving my skills en route to the championship.

Because these minigames are relatively surface level, they have diminishing returns once you reach a certain skill level. While I loved it when my tennis opponents started challenging me to use better angles and my fellow golfers stopped air-mailing their tee shots straight into the trees, higher-skill opponents rarely energized me; instead, sports like bowling that include fun, off-the-wall variants were my favorite ladders to ascend, as they truly put my skills to the test as I was forced to adapt to moving obstacles and ramps. I just wish Nintendo injected more fun twists to keep the experiences fresh.

After completing each activity, you’re awarded Spocco Points, which you use to unlock themed customization elements for your characters. Cartoon-like Sportsmates replace the iconic Mii characters introduced in Wii Sports, but you can still use your Mii if you choose. Though I have fondness for Mii characters, after two entries featuring the more expressive, customizable Sportsmates, I now prefer those character models.

Nintendo Switch Sports Resort delivers a capable compilation of quick-hit sports minigames that can be easily enjoyed regardless of how many people are holding Joy-Cons. Though I certainly have my destination sports, there’s not a loser in the bunch. Island Crown mode does a great job expanding the single-player options, but this package’s legs are in its multiplayer experience. And since Switch Sports Resort is chock-full of easily approachable minigames, I can see it becoming a cherished inclusion in the rotation of party mainstays.

#4268. Football Manager 27 will be free to play for five days before its full release, in "industry first" for a game of its kind

So you can see Wenger you like it or not.

You can play Football Manager 27 on PC and Mac for free this year for five full days later this month, in a first for the series and apparently an "industry first" for games of its kind.

The free period itself will start on 29th October at 6pm GMT, and run until its typical paid 'launch preview' period starts on the 3rd November, which is available to those who pre-order the game before its full launch on 10th November.

"Making an annual title free to play with all game modes available is believed to be an industry first and is a move that continues Sports Interactive's efforts to support its community of players," a studio press release says.

FM27's pre-orders, meanwhile, will then only open alongside the free play period, where players can buy with a 10 percent discount to get access to the aforementioned launch preview. Your saves will also continue across all of these periods into the full game, which is a nice touch, and likewise for those playing on Xbox Game Pass, too.

The news comes in an unusually quiet pre-launch period for developer Sports Interactive, and after the especially rough year of FM26, which despite steady player numbers still sits at a 'Mostly Negative' Steam user score, almost a full year after its launch.

That game was the first in two years, after Sports Interactive took the extraordinary step of cancelling the release of FM25 while it moved the game over to the Unity game engine from its own internal one. The result was new systems that the developer says weren't possible in previous versions of the game - notably the quite sophisticated system of splitting out in- and out-of-possession formations and tactics - at the expense of a huge number of classic features the developer couldn't get working in time. That, and a reworked "cards and tiles" user interface that I and an awful lot of players found a bit of a nightmare to navigate.

It all makes for a huge year for the franchise. FM27 promises "an improved interface with more intuitive navigation, creating a more immersive experience," as well as several new features such as Tactical Tweaks, which supplement the old, returning touchline shouts system. Whether they come off is likely to have a major impact on fans' trust in the studio after a difficult couple of years. This five-day free period at least suggests Sports Interactive is feeling confident.

#4266. Samsung's Dolby Atmos Soundbar (With a Wireless Subwoofer) Is $110 Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Samsung’s HW-Q600F soundbar is down to $389.99 at Amazon from $499.99, a $110 discount that brings it to its lowest price ever, according to price trackers. This 2025 model sits in the middle of Samsung’s Q-Series lineup and pairs a sleek, angular bar with a subwoofer in a 3.1.2 setup, where the bar’s nine drivers cover the left, center, and right channels as well as two up-firing height channels. Those up-firing drivers let it handle Dolby Atmos and other object-based audio correctly, an advantage over Samsung’s 5.1-channel HW-B750F, though it gives up the extra audio channels, app support, and broader connectivity of the pricier HW-Q800F.

For everyday listening, the HW-Q600F performs particularly well, with balanced tuning across the stereo and center channels that keeps dialogue clear without requiring much adjustment. It also preserves the dynamics of a soundtrack, so an explosion still lands with more force than the conversation before it. Dolby Atmos also brings a modest sense of height, and effects track cleanly from left to right. Surround sound, on the other hand, is less convincing, since the soundbar doesn’t include rear satellite speakers and downmixes surround formats, which limits its ability to convincingly place effects behind you. You do get a graphic equalizer and sound presets for adjusting the audio to your preferences, though without a companion app, you’ll be making those changes through the included remote.

On the connectivity side, the bar includes an HDMI input, so you can plug a game console into it and pass video through to your TV, while an optical port covers older devices. That said, HDMI audio latency runs slightly high, potentially requiring an adjustment in your TV's settings to keep dialogue synchronized with what's happening on screen. As for wireless connectivity, it is limited to Bluetooth, meaning no wifi streaming or multi-room audio. Also, Samsung's SpaceFit Sound Pro room correction and Q-Symphony integration, which lets the TV’s speakers play alongside the bar, only work with compatible Samsung sets.

#4265. A tiny defect in a crystal reveals a big quantum surprise

When light strikes a tiny imperfection inside a material such as diamond or silicon carbide, it can transfer energy to electrons and push them into an excited state. These imperfections, known as spin defects, could become valuable building blocks for future quantum technologies, including quantum sensors. Because they can function as stable qubits whose states can be read, scientists are eager to understand exactly how they behave. However, predicting how their energy changes over time has remained a major challenge.

Researchers at the University of Chicago Pritzker School of Molecular Engineering (UChicago PME) have now developed a computational method that provides a more complete picture of what happens when these defects interact with light. Their approach can simulate the entire optical cycle, including a particularly difficult process known as internal conversion.

The findings, published in Physical Review Letters, could help scientists design better materials for quantum technologies. The method may also prove useful for studying materials involved in renewable energy conversion and storage.

"This internal conversion process is not only relevant to spin defects -- it's also related to processes of interest to energy technologies," said Giulia Galli, the Liew Family Professor of Electronic Structure and Simulations in UChicago PME and the Department of Chemistry and the senior author of the study. "That's one of the reasons this work is so exciting."

When a laser excites a spin defect, the defect can return to its original, lower-energy state through several different processes. It might release a photon of light, transition between states with different spins, or transfer its energy into vibrations of the surrounding atoms.

That final pathway, called internal conversion, has been especially difficult for scientists to model accurately. Although the fundamental equations of quantum mechanics can describe the other two processes, calculating how energy is lost through atomic vibrations presents substantial computational obstacles.

"Although the equations are known, it's hard to describe the process accurately because of computational difficulties," said Stefano Villani, a UChicago PME postdoctoral researcher and the study's first author. "You need to account for complex interactions between the electrons and surrounding atoms, and that's challenging in realistic solids represented by models with hundreds or thousands of atoms"

To overcome this problem, Galli, Villani, and Yu Jin combined several computational techniques that their research group had developed over many years. Jin, a former graduate student in Galli's group, is now a postdoctoral scholar at the Flatiron Institute in New York.

The researchers used mathematical descriptions known as many-body wavefunctions to represent the excited states of spin defects. Through exact mathematical derivations, they determined how strongly each excited state interacts with the movements of nearby atoms.

This allowed them to calculate how individual electronic states connect with all the possible vibrational configurations of hundreds of surrounding atoms, rather than having to analyze those configurations individually.

"In the past, a clear mathematical derivation and efficient algorithms were not available," explained Yu Jin. "The new derivation came together after years of work on the optical cycle of spin defects."

The researchers incorporated their new framework into WEST, an open-source simulation program optimized for graphics processing units (GPUs). Galli's group has spent the past decade developing WEST through the Midwest Integrated Center for Computational Materials (MICCoM), which has received funding from the U.S. Department of Energy's Office of Basic Energy Sciences since 2015.

A Surprising Discovery Inside Silicon Carbide

To test their approach, the researchers first examined a nitrogen-vacancy center in diamond, a type of crystal defect widely studied for quantum applications.

Their calculations closely matched recent spectroscopy measurements. For triplet states, internal conversion contributed relatively little to energy loss. In singlet states, however, it was the primary mechanism responsible for releasing energy. Both findings agreed with what scientists had previously expected.

The results changed when the team investigated a divacancy defect in silicon carbide.

Their calculations revealed that internal conversion has a much greater influence on the lifetime of the defect's excited triplet state than earlier models had accounted for. This previously underestimated contribution helped explain a long-standing disagreement between theoretical predictions and experimental observations.

"We were expecting to see an effect, but of a smaller magnitude," Galli said. "What we found is that this internal conversion process turns out to be more important than we thought."

Understanding this unexpected energy-loss pathway could help scientists more accurately predict the behavior of silicon carbide defects and improve their potential use as qubits in future quantum devices.

A New Tool for Quantum Computing and Energy Research

With the framework now available through WEST, researchers at other institutions can begin applying it to their own studies. However, Galli noted that the simulations still demand considerable computing power.

The team is exploring ways to reduce those requirements, including the use of artificial intelligence.

"We are working on ways to integrate machine learning into excited states calculations to further improve the efficiency of our method," Villani said.

Meanwhile, the scientists are expanding their approach to investigate spin defects in silicon, as well as materials used in energy technologies where internal conversion influences how energy is transferred or lost.

By addressing one of the most difficult parts of modeling how spin defects respond to light, the researchers have made it possible to predict their behavior more completely and accurately.

"We provided the missing piece of the puzzle needed to completely describe the optical cycle of spin defects," Galli said. "Our work can now be used to make more accurate predictions about many materials of interest for quantum technologies," added Yu Jin.

Materials provided by University of Chicago. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#4263. Final Fantasy Resonance Review - On Its Own Wavelength

RPG publisher Square Enix has been doing the Lord's work lately by rescuing their discontinued mobile games and rehydrating them into full-fat RPG experiences. Last year's Octopath Traveler 0 is one such triumph, and it's safe to say Final Fantasy Resonance is another victory for reusing assets instead of throwing them in the garbage. More importantly, it's a great RPG experience with a fun cast and character customization options that will make fans of the Final Fantasy V job system rabid with delight.

Resonance is based on the first season of the now-defunct Final Fantasy Brave Exvius mobile game, which ran four chapters in total from 2016 to 2025, but Resonance is no mere port. For starters, it received an HD-2D injection that gussied up Exvius's already-impressive sprites. Resonance wears the HD-2D aesthetic confidently; Square Enix has obviously listened to past criticisms of its beloved graphical style, and it paid off. Resonance is an HD-2D game, but its highly detailed character sprites and occasional jaw-dropping pixel-based "cinema" scene differentiate it from the Octopath Traveler series and the Dragon Quest HD-2D remakes.

Resonance's distinctions are its greatest strength, in fact. It's easy to assume at first blush that its story about a boy and his best friend is nothing but a table to prop retro Final Fantasy references upon. It's true that a key part of Resonance's gameplay involves summoning "Visions" into battle, said visions being projections of characters from other Final Fantasy games. But that's all they are: Projections. They teach transferable skills, à la Final Fantasy V's job system. They can offer characters permanent stat boosts as they level up, like Final Fantasy VI's espers. And when conditions are met, they can be summoned to perform a super attack via a CGI spectacle that will remind you Square Enix have been masters of game cinema direction since day one.

Each Resonance character can equip a Vision that lends them their powers in a fight, and also grants passive upgrades and skills. Rain, for example, is a heavy hitter with innate fire attacks, but if he wears Cecil Harvey as a backpack, he also gets access to light and dark magic. Cecil gets stronger as they fight together, and bestows skills that can be equipped as wanted. If you're a Final Fantasy V job-head, you've come home.

But Final Fantasy Resonance is Rain's story despite its many callbacks to past games. Yes, Rain is an amalgamation of past Final Fantasy heroes, from Cloud's spiky yellow hair to Tidus's daddy issues, to Zidane's sunny attitude (with a hint of shadow), but that just makes him easier to root for. Rain's roommate, Lasswell, contains notes of my beloved Kain Highwind from Final Fantasy IV, but there's a troubled nobility to him that's purely his own. They're gradually joined by a cast of characters who click with each other (and thus, with me) thanks in part to comedic side-discussions delivered with quality voice acting. References to past Final Fantasy games are doled with clever subtlety. As a Final Fantasy XIV fan, I had to peel myself off the ceiling more than once when I encountered some choice shoutouts.

Resonance's battle system grants it another distinction. It's a classic turn-based system that shows off who's next in line to attack, like Final Fantasy X. Every character brings a fighting style and elemental flavor to the party. Lasswell is a samurai who favors ice, Jake is a gunner attuned to the wind, and Lid will rain the fattest of steel chocobos upon your head in an earth-based attack.

Resonance is a snappy game, which might be its most welcome trait. Fast travel is generous, and dungeons are conquered in just the right amount of time. You're looking at a 40-hour experience, give or take, which is downright digestible for an RPG in this day and age. On the flipside, Rain's world lacks depth. Its towns are beautiful, unique, and filled with characters and quest-givers who are worth talking to, but the overworld connective tissue is thin and its dungeons lack pizzazz. An auto-battle option would lessen the frustration of random encounters, but said encounters don't pop up nearly as often as they do in ancestral Final Fantasy games. Plus, the random battles are spaced in a way that makes grinding unnecessary unless you're maxing out a Vision, which I appreciate, but sometimes you're just trying to get to a place.

I went into Resonance expecting to like it for its salute to Final Fantasy characters across the decades, but I wound up loving the game for showing me a whole new canon. In a time when mainline Final Fantasy is still figuring itself out, Final Fantasy Resonance is easy to recommend. It has one foot planted on the solid foundation of Final Fantasy's history, but with its rich graphics and likeable cast of characters it builds up from that history instead of merely pillaging it.
