#4227. Your blood could reveal inflammatory bowel disease 10 years before diagnosis

Scientists have identified hidden changes in the immune system that can appear up to 10 years before a person is diagnosed with inflammatory bowel disease (IBD). The findings, presented at UEG Week 2026, suggest that certain antibody patterns in the blood could eventually help doctors identify people at risk of developing the condition and even predict how severe their disease might become.

Inflammatory bowel disease refers to a group of long-term disorders, including Crohn's disease (CD) and ulcerative colitis (UC), that cause persistent inflammation in the digestive tract. These conditions develop when the immune system responds abnormally, contributing to inflammation and damage in the intestines.

Scientists increasingly believe that IBD begins developing years before it is officially diagnosed. However, the immune system changes that take place during this early, largely hidden period have remained poorly understood.

Tracking Immune Changes a Decade Before Diagnosis

To investigate what happens before IBD is diagnosed, researchers examined 2,000 blood serum samples collected from 500 people. The participants included 200 who eventually developed Crohn's disease, 200 who developed ulcerative colitis, and 100 healthy individuals of comparable age and sex.

Blood samples were available from the time of diagnosis and from approximately two, four, and 10 years earlier. This allowed the researchers to look backward through a decade of immune activity and identify changes that emerged before the diseases were recognized.

The team analyzed antibody reactions to approximately 357,000 antigens associated with bacteria, viruses, food, and the immune system itself. Antigens are substances that the immune system can recognize and respond to, while antibodies are proteins produced by the immune system that help identify specific targets.

Across all 2,000 samples, the researchers detected 123,980 unique antibodies.

A striking pattern emerged. People who later developed Crohn's disease or ulcerative colitis had significantly less antibody diversity than healthy participants, meaning their immune systems showed a narrower range of antibody responses. Distinctive changes could be detected in both diseases as far back as 10 years before diagnosis.

Different Blood Signals Reveal Crohn's Disease and Ulcerative Colitis

The researchers also found that Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis were associated with different immune signatures.

Among people who later developed Crohn's disease, antibody responses to herpesviruses and bacterial flagellin were elevated. Flagellin is a protein that forms part of the tail-like structures some bacteria use to move.

These elevated responses were especially noticeable in individuals who eventually developed complicated Crohn's disease or disease affecting the ileum, the final section of the small intestine.

At the same time, antibody responses to encapsulated bacteria gradually decreased as diagnosis approached. These bacteria are surrounded by protective outer coatings that can help them evade immune defenses.

People who later developed ulcerative colitis showed a somewhat different pattern. Their antibody responses to herpesviruses and autoantigens increased, while responses to encapsulated pathogens declined. Autoantigens are components of the body's own tissues that can mistakenly become targets of an immune response.

Together, these findings suggest that the immune system undergoes measurable changes many years before inflammatory bowel disease is diagnosed.

AI Identifies Hidden Disease Risk Years in Advance

The researchers then used machine learning, a form of artificial intelligence that identifies patterns in complex data, to determine whether these antibody signatures could distinguish people who would eventually develop IBD from healthy individuals.

Using blood antibody patterns from up to 10 years before diagnosis, the models distinguished future Crohn's disease cases from healthy controls with an area under the curve (AUC) of 0.90. For ulcerative colitis, the corresponding value was 0.84.

AUC is a measure of how well a model distinguishes between two groups. A score of 0.5 indicates performance equivalent to chance, while 1.0 represents perfect discrimination.

The researchers also discovered that antibody patterns could help distinguish people who would develop complicated Crohn's disease from those whose disease would remain less complicated. This distinction was possible approximately 10 years before diagnosis, with an AUC of 0.85.

These findings raise the possibility that blood antibody testing could eventually help identify not only who is at increased risk of IBD, but also which patients may need more intensive treatment.

Commenting on the findings, lead author Dr. Arno Bourgonje said: "It is fascinating to see that complicated CD at diagnosis is associated with a distinct antibody profile years before the first clinical symptoms and is not simply the consequence of disease progression after diagnosis. Finding these patterns so early points to the potential of antibody profiling to support earlier risk detection, distinguish between disease subtypes and help identify patients who may benefit from more intensive therapy as early as possible."

Could a Blood Test Detect Gut Disease 10 Years Early?

Although the results offer compelling evidence that IBD has a detectable early immune signature, the researchers emphasize that the findings are not yet ready for use in routine medical screening.

The study establishes an important proof of principle, but additional research is necessary to determine whether these antibody patterns can reliably predict disease in broader populations.

Researchers must also establish whether antibody testing provides meaningful benefits beyond existing genetic information, clinical assessments, and other blood-based measurements. Any potential screening test would need to be accurate, affordable, reproducible, and ultimately shown to improve patient outcomes.

"The next priority is to validate these signatures in independent, more diverse and prospectively followed populations," concluded Dr. Bourgonje. "We also need to determine their added value alongside genetic, clinical and other blood-based markers, translate the findings into a reproducible and affordable targeted assay and, ultimately, demonstrate that using such a test actually leads to better clinical outcomes."

If future studies confirm these results, blood-based immune profiling could offer doctors a new way to recognize inflammatory bowel disease risk years before diagnosis, distinguish between disease subtypes, and identify patients who might benefit from earlier intervention.

#4226. How to Preorder the 'HomeView' and Apple's Other New Smart Devices

Apple announced a lot of new devices during its "Welcome Home" event yesterday. The Apple HomeView, the company's first smart display hub, stole the show, with its squared-off seven-inch display and its "oversized Apple Watch meets iMac G4" design, but Apple is also collaborating with LG and Schneider on a collection of smart devices, like lights, doorbells, and cameras, that the HomeView can control. Plus, the company also announced updates to existing products, including the Apple TV 4K and HomePod mini.

None of these items is currently available to pick up in-store. However, many—but not all—of them are already available to preorder, if you're interested in being among the first to try them. Here's how you can reserve the HomeView, Apple TV 4K, and HomePod mini 2 today.

Apple's HomeView is a unique product for the company. With it, you can control your entire smart home, chat with Siri AI, check your messages, take FaceTime calls, and play music, among other tasks. Though it's generally far more limited than an iPad, it's the only device of the two to offer individualized experiences per user: It can recognize up to six people in your household and display personalized information (like Messages) for each. You can preorder the HomeView from stores like Apple, Amazon, and Best Buy. However, if you opt for Amazon, you can take $10 off the Space Gray and Starlight standard models:

Just remember: If you're buying a wall-mounted option, you'll need to buy the actual mount separately. If you buy the Power Mount USB-C wall connector, that'll add $59.99 to your total, while the Power Mount AC-wired option will add $79.99.

Apple's newest Apple TV 4K looks a lot like the previous model, but what's inside has changed. This model uses Apple's A19 chip and can connect to up to four HomePod devices at once. The new Siri Remote supports Precision Finding through Find My, and has a speaker you can ping if you lose it in your couch. Apple doesn't always make its product names all that clear. For example, the latest iteration of Apple TV 4K is simply called "Apple TV 4K." If you're looking to buy one, don't just buy any "Apple TV 4K" you see. Instead, make sure it is the fourth-generation model. You'll have two clues there, besides the (4th-gen) label: First, it isn't currently available to purchase, so you'll only be able to preorder it; second, it costs $299, $100 more than the third-gen Apple TV 4K.

The HomePod mini 2 comes in refreshed colors that just so happen to match Apple's iMacs. The new speakers run on the Apple S10 chip and come with a new "Sound Glow" feature that can pulse to the music with colors that match the track's album art. They can also act as default speakers for iMacs over Wi-Fi 6e. Amazon doesn't have Apple's latest HomePod mini device ready to preorder. If you search for it, you'll only find previous-generation speakers available to purchase now—and only renewed versions, at that. In addition to Apple, Best Buy also has the new HomePod mini 2 available to preorder in all five colors:

The Apple Home Collection is pricey, but Apple won't be taking your money for it just yet. While the company has a landing page for the new smart devices, including the $349.95 LG Smart Deadbolt Lock and $399.95 LG Floodlight Cam, none of them are officially available to preorder or purchase. Apple says some of these devices will be available in December, while others will arrive in early 2027, though it doesn't say which devices will launch when.

#4225. Half-Life 3 speculation reignited by Valve dev mentioning three sweet little rumored codenamey letters amid technical chat about video game noises

Wake up. There's Half-Life 3 chatter occurring again. This time because, about an hour into a panel about sounds in video games, a Valve composer has uttered three eyebrow-raising letters. HLX. In an industry rife with acronyms, what's so special about this one? Well, it's a rumoured codename for the next entry in the aforementioned series about a man with a crowbar, for one.

"Basically, that's one of the three pieces that come together to make HDR that you can easily optimise," Valve music designer Mike Morasky said during a panel at GameSoundCon, answering a regular old question about the techincal ins and out of managing sound in games. The panel had been running for about an hour when he said this, so clearly nothing that'd get people out of their seats was in the offing.

"In fact, I think Deadlock has their own version of HDR," he continued. "HLX has its own version, so on and so forth." AHA, voices screamed around the world. He said the thing. HLX, in case you're not on top of the list of acronyms that should set you off yapping about Valve games, previously appeared in a data string from a Counter-Strike 2 update and looks like it could turn out to be a codename for a future game from Gabe and co. That's going off Half-Life: Alyx's codename having been HLVR.

If it was HLXXX, I imagine one would have to assume it to be a possible codename for a Gordon Freeman sex tape or a Half-Life collaboration with Vin Diesel, star of the 2002 film XXX. I don't know about you, but either would excite me greatly.

Morasky's co-panelist David Feise doesn't look to have been subtle in sarcastically pointing out the former's slip of the tongue to him via some cheeky whispering while the two were still up in stage. Morasky also looks to have plenty of form for this sort of tongue slippage in relation to HLX.

Does any of this tell us anything other than potentially confirming once more that Half-Life 3 exists? No, but hey, it's something. One thing we do know is that ex-Valve writer Chet Faliszek has said that he wouldn't be interested in helping pen a Half-Life 3 if indeed one is hiding in the ether to catch out musicians whose mouths sometimes move faster than their brains (as happens to all of us).

#4224. World of Warcraft: Forever's beta has been extended, as community members mourn the loss of their hunter pets

World of Warcraft: Forever's beta has been extended until this Friday, much to the joy of the community of early players.

Announced on X by World of Warcraft's Associate production director Clayton Stone, this extension will allow those who have hopped into the MMO for their level 1-30 journey to spend just a little bit more time before servers go down this weekend.

In addition Blizzard is hosting a server stress test today, allowing players - including those who otherwise wouldn't have access to the beta - a chance to hop on and bush the game to its limits. After the servers go down on Friday, WoW Forever lovers will have a chance to reserve a character name next week, ahead of the full launch on the 4th November.

This obviously is great news for those who can't get enough of World of Warcraft: Forever, but there's a funny consequence to this extension. On World of Warcraft community hubs, players made lovely farewell posts to the game ahead of the original beta end date. Like this one, where Reddit user Roodster123 sat their gnome down overlooking the Stormwind docks in a post titled "This is where I leave you little one. We had a good run. Until we meet again."

Another user No_Staff_6299 made a similar post titled "Wait for me Kitten, I'll be back soon." In it, we can see their character crying and dismissing their pet spider, never to be seen again. A hunter's pet is rather important in World of Warcraft, so if they want to play the beta some more, they'll need to capture a new one - no doubt it'll be a pet with far fewer fond memories attached. The comments on that post are full with other hunters sharing their similar stories. "Lets pour one out for u/No_Staff_6299" wrote user Thirteenera, in reaction to the news.

While it's maybe a little late to hop on now, World of Warcraft: Forever's beta gave an excellent first impression to the upcoming MMO. That obviously is brilliant for those excited to dive back into Azeroth, and equally tragic for those of us unable to get time off work.

#4223. Crimson Desert PC specs don't keep resolution and framerate targets a secret, and they don't use upscaling for everything either

Finally, a PC specification list that actually indicates resolution and framerate targets, and doesn't leave console players behind.

Crimson Desert is a little over one week away from release, and developer Pearl Abyss has delivered the official, final list of specs for the game. The twist is that the specs not only include useful information for PC players, they also cover similarly relevant details for console players, who traditionally don’t get mentioned in those reveals.

The specs are decently detailed, but more surprisingly - except for a single instance - they don’t include upscaling on PC, another thing games have gotten too comfortable using, even with their lowest specs.

On the official Crimson Desert website, developer Pearl Abyss has delivered a chunky post breaking down five different sets of PC requirements for the upcoming open-world action RPG. Each set indicates the graphics preset being used - and crucially, the resolution and framerate targets you can expect with each.

With the exception of the Minimum spec, all other presets do not appear to rely on upscaling of any kind. They do not say, however, whether ray tracing is being used with any of them, which is a feature that will throw all of those specs out of whack.

That same transparency also extends to non-PC platforms, which traditionally get few, if any, details about their own resolution and framerate targets. The console set of specs are extensive, just like PC, covering both PlayStation, and Xbox.

It’s granular enough to indicate which upscaling tech is being used, where applicable. This is relevant for owners of a PS5 Pro, a console that has access to Sony’s PSSR upscaling, alongside the standard AMD FSR tech the base console - and Xbox - use.

The developer has also revealed the base resolution for each mode across each console, and the resolution the image will be upscaled to using whichever tech is being utilised.

And, if you’re one of the five people who play games on Mac, you’ll be thrilled to know that the specs also include a section for Mac devices, split between the different Mac chips. The quality preset being used with each is not clear, however.

Finally, there's a set of specs for the Rog Xbox Ally, covering both the X and S models.

Crimson Desert arrives March 20 on PC, PS5, and Xbox Series X/S. You can expect more coverage from us at release and beyond.

How to get and use Refinement Tokens in Crimson Desert
